Abstract (英文): Action-conditioned video prediction lets robots anticipate the visual consequences of their actions for planning and policy evaluation. Existing predictors denoise each future from noise without using their own estimate of it, which limits the quality of the predicted future, in particular of the manipulated objects, and requires many network calls. We present TwoPass, a self-conditioned training-and-inference framework that keeps the standard flow-matching objective. Self-Generated Draft Training supplies a detached clean-video estimate from the shared network as conditioning at the same noisy state. A Refinement-Only Late Adapter applies a dedicated residual transformation when a draft is present. At inference, Draft-Reuse Iterative Refinement obtains both a solver update and the next draft from each velocity prediction, requiring as few as 4 function evaluations (NFE) per video chunk. Training samples discrete solver-query noise levels and updates the pretrained backbone. Extensive experiments on Bridge and Language Table show that TwoPass achieves state-of-the-art results with 4 NFE, far fewer than the 50-59 calls of existing predictors. Code: https://github.com/PlantPotatoOnMoon/TwoPass.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We identify, for the first time, a new model modification attack--the cross-task model repurposing attack--that can render fingerprints generated by existing model fingerprinting approaches inapplicable. To address this challenge, we propose UniReFP--a novel unified model fingerprinting framework applicable to diverse vision models across different tasks--that utilizes ownership evidence in a shared class-level semantic space rather than original task-specific output space, enabling effective ownership verification when a protected model is repurposed across tasks with heterogeneous output formats. Specifically, UniReFP constructs a surrogate classifier by repurposing the protected model, enabling fingerprints to be generated independently of the original task-dependent output space of the protected model. Moreover, it optimizes grouped fingerprints to encode ownership evidence: fingerprints within the same group are encouraged to induce consistent semantic responses and intermediate representations on the surrogate classifier, while producing dispersed responses on independently trained reference classifiers. During verification, UniReFP uses a task-aware semantic projection mechanism to map heterogeneous suspect-model outputs into unified semantic-presence vectors, and determines ownership by measuring inner-group consistency. Extensive experimental results validate the effectiveness of UniReFP, consistently showing superior performance under cross-task repurposing attacks, commonly studied model modification attacks, and even their combinations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An assistant monitoring a person performing a procedure is most useful when it can detect mistakes while there is still time to recover. This requires judging actions against task instructions as the video unfolds, identifying the attempted step, locating it in time, and assessing its execution. Existing online detectors do not use the instruction text, while instruction-conditioned methods operate offline. In this paper, we study online, instruction-conditioned mistake detection in egocentric videos. Using only observed video, task instructions, and prior predictions, our model identifies and localizes completed action attempts and classifies them as correct, modified, slip, corrections, or additions; omitted steps are determined after the video ends. We compare a modular pipeline, with separate step identification, temporal localization, and execution verification, to a joint model that predicts all outputs in a single step. To support supervised learning from mistakes, we repartition EgoPER to include erroneous videos in all splits. The approaches achieve similar step-segmentation F1@0.5 (93.6% and 92.1%), while the joint model achieves higher binary mistake-detection F1@0.5 (84.1% vs. 80.7%), with its largest gain on slips (56.4% vs. 37.5%). Component-level analysis suggests that error propagation between stages contributes to the modular approach's lower detection score. On test videos containing mistakes, both designs outperform released AEM and ESTANet checkpoints under our evaluation protocol.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Extending multimodal large language models (MLLMs) from images to videos faces substantial memory and latency challenges due to the massive number of visual tokens. These challenges have driven the development of numerous visual token compression methods and more efficient LLM architectures. However, existing compression methods are often evaluated with different training setups, tasks, and models, making fair comparison difficult. Meanwhile, the growing use of hybrid Mamba-Transformer architectures raises questions about whether compression strategies transfer across backbones. We present VidToC, a controlled evaluation framework for representative compression algorithms, LLM backbones, and time encoding strategies, covering training-free and fine-tuned settings on three complementary video understanding tasks. Our results reveal task-dependent compression behavior and characterize the effects of compression placement, time encoding, and backbone choice. These findings provide empirical guidance for designing and selecting video token compression strategies across tasks and training settings.

Abstract (英文): Hybrid quantum-classical learning architectures provide a promising framework for integrating quantum feature transformations into complex data-processing tasks. Among these transformations, the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) offers a structured approach to feature processing, motivating an investigation of how its circuit configuration influences predictive performance, quantum resource requirements, and noise robustness. We present a hybrid PointNet-quantum neural network architecture for LiDAR point-cloud classification, combining classical feature extraction, amplitude encoding, and a variational quantum circuit. Five Fourier-processing configurations are systematically evaluated: No-QFT, Full-QFT-After, QFT-no-swap, IQFT-After, and Trainable-QFT. Experiments on KITTI, nuScenes, and PandaSet across three random seeds assess classification performance, class-level behavior, circuit complexity, and sensitivity to the IBM FakeManilaV2 noise model. Under ideal simulation, IQFT-After achieves the highest weighted F1-score on KITTI (0.857 $\pm$ 0.033), compared with 0.838 $\pm$ 0.023 for No-QFT. Removing the final SWAP gates in the QFT circuit reduces circuit complexity while preserving the Pauli-Z measurement features up to a fixed permutation that can be absorbed by the classification head. Under quantum noise, the circuit ranking changes, with IQFT-After exhibiting greater performance degradation and No-QFT and Trainable-QFT showing stronger robustness. Per-class analysis further identifies dataset-dependent differences in minority-class performance. These results demonstrate that Fourier-processing configuration influences both classification behavior and noise sensitivity, highlighting the role of circuit structure and hardware-aware evaluation in hybrid quantum learning for three-dimensional point-cloud data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An age prompt can change how a vision-language model (VLM) rates the emotion evoked by an image. Does the change predict how people of different ages rate that same image? We evaluate 17 VLMs on 900 OASIS images using young-, middle-, and older-adult prompts and estimate image-specific age differences from ratings by 822 participants. Without an age cue, models closely track average human valence (median Pearson $r=0.891$). Under an older-adult prompt, 16 of 17 models assign lower valence on average than under a young-adult prompt. Yet after adjusting for image pleasantness, correlations between model and human age effects range from $-0.030$ to $0.093$, against an estimated ceiling of $0.595$. The discrepancy is clearest on 54 sensitive images rated as pleasant. Across models, the older prompt reduces valence by $0.845$ points on average, while older participants rate these images $0.443$ points higher than younger participants. Providing human ratings from other images improves agreement on the direction of age differences, although image-specific fidelity remains modest on 600 held-out images ($r_{\mathrm{part}}=0.105$). These results show why evaluations of demographic personalization in foundation models need human comparisons at the level of individual stimuli. Project page available at https://shahriyar-zaman.github.io/age-conditioned-emotion-reasoning/

Abstract (英文): World action models (WAMs) couple generative video modeling with robotic action prediction but typically require large video and action transformers, limiting deployment efficiency. We present LoopedWAM, a compact WAM that recurrently applies a shared six-block core in each expert, executing 30 transformer layers while storing only 12 blocks per expert. To account for their asymmetric inference costs, the video and action experts can operate at different depths: visual features are computed once per query, whereas action computation repeats at every sampling step. We further propose elastic self-distillation, which trains a single elastic checkpoint, LoopedWAM-E, across action-depth and sampling-step budgets. With only 0.58B policy parameters and no robot pretraining, LoopedWAM achieves 96.1% success on LIBERO, 91.9% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 52.2% on RoboCasaGR1, while using $7.4\times$ less peak GPU memory than Fast-WAM under matched inference measurements. On LIBERO, LoopedWAM-E achieves 96.0% success in 27.6 ms or 95.2% in 24.7 ms on an H100, providing multiple accuracy-latency operating points from a single checkpoint; with two sampling steps and four action loops, it stays within 0.5 points of LoopedWAM on all three benchmarks. Ablations demonstrate that weight-shared depth preserves competitive performance, while aggressive action-depth reduction requires explicit budget-aware training. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, LoopedWAM succeeds in 71.3% of 80 trials across four tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Current multi-shot video generators render only limited durations per pass, requiring long movies to be stitched from local segments across which narrative progression and visual identity drift. To synthesize long-horizon movies with Coherence across Time, we present CaT-Movie, a multi-shot generation system consisting of a global story planner and a local window renderer. The planner expands user requests via a large language model to preserve, infer, and complete story details, while deterministic code maintains the resulting storyboard across shots to compile self-contained shot prompts and index reference frames from generated history. To execute this guidance, the renderer applies reference-routed sparse self-attention and shot-wise cross-attention to route identities from the indexed frames while isolating per-shot textual conditions. Given the scarcity of high-quality open-source multi-shot data, we construct a dataset of over 350K film shots annotated with semantic state transitions, entity grounding, and multi-granularity requests. We further build CaT-MBench, a benchmark on held-out films with the same annotations. It gives every baseline its native format and counts cross-shot consistency only when a cut opens a new camera setup, preventing repeated framing from passing as consistency. On CaT-MBench, CaT-Movie attains the highest average score and the most consistent characters among all methods. The project page is available at https://jinplu.github.io/CaT-Movie/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Blind image deconvolution (BID) is an ill-posed inverse problem that aims to jointly recover a sharp image and blur kernel from a single degraded observation. Under nontrivial and high noise, which is common in photography and scientific imaging such as microscopy, BID remains particularly challenging, as existing methods suffer from noise overfitting and inaccurate kernel estimation. Existing training-data-free learning methods for BID, such as those based on the deep image prior (DIP), often utilize external guidance by including model-based estimates as additional loss terms, which underexploits the information these estimates carry especially under nontrivial noise. To address these challenges, we propose DAWN, a training-data-free DIP framework that alternates between injecting model-based estimates directly into the model input and refining this imperfect estimate with the network's own prior. To provide more reliable kernel estimates for these injections, we apply autoencoding regularization to both the image and kernel networks. On natural and scientific images under varying levels of Gaussian and Poisson noise, DAWN outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both image and kernel quality (achieving up to +4.5 dB over the next best baseline on the Levin dataset at $σ= 0.1$). Without any training images, DAWN remains highly competitive with a state-of-the-art diffusion model on FFHQ, despite the latter being trained on a massive number of in-distribution images.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Do video world models learn physical laws without a physics loss, or merely imitate motion? Overall motion and appearance scores cannot settle it. We investigate this through nonlinear forced response: an excitation shakes the support of a suspended object. We introduce dual visual-numeric conditioning and adapt six image-to-video backbones to predict this motion using only the flow-matching loss. Wan predicts motion accurately and generalizes to unseen excitations, objects, poses, and scenes. Counterfactual tests suggest capabilities beyond replaying training videos. But what laws have they learned? We design three tiers of tests using Taylor expansions of the dynamics and geometry. All six reproduce the linear response, including resonance; five capture higher-order dynamics, including nonlinear softening. Remarkably, softening is barely present in training: only 0.74% of the swing's squared displacement. Extensions to a double pendulum and a rocking block show sustained dynamics are learned more faithfully than brief impacts. Yet learned dynamics may fail to appear when state estimation or propagation goes wrong. We turn these tests and findings into strategies to improve video training and generation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Frozen diffusion descriptors retrieve strong semantic correspondence candidates, yet cosine nearest-neighbor matching can select incorrect locations despite valid high-ranking alternatives. Across DIFT-SD2.1 and DiTF-FLUX on SPair-71k, AP-10K, and SOCO (CC, SOC, Cross-SOC), 41-69% of Top-1 errors retain a PCK@0.10-valid match among the original Top-5 spatial peaks, revealing a persistent candidate-selection bottleneck. We find that descriptor rankings and current-pair relational evidence can favor different candidates: reciprocal diffusion Query/Key associations and pretrained RoMa transport exhibit distinct, partly complementary preferences. To incorporate this evidence into matching decisions, we introduce RelGeo, a Pair-Conditioned Relational Calibration framework expressing heterogeneous relations as shared source-target coordinates. Spectral Relational Coordinates (SRC) encode reciprocal diffusion affinities, while Warp Identity Coordinates (WIC) encode reliability-weighted bidirectional transport endpoints. Their inner products augment frozen descriptor similarity while preserving full-domain cosine nearest-neighbor matching without additional semantic-correspondence training. RelGeo improves all ten evaluations by an average of 5.51 PCK@0.10 points; fixed-candidate reranking and pair-misalignment controls corroborate improved selection and dependence on current-pair relational alignment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a storm can head north or south, a forecast down the middle may score better under squared error than either plausible path. The same paradox appears when video generators are tested as world models: fewer denoising steps can raise physics scores even as videos blur or stop moving early. Why? Physics-IQ compares each generated video with one recording, although a scene can have multiple valid futures. An exact decomposition shows its scores can reward reduced variation among generated futures, even when their distribution becomes less accurate. We introduce Kernel Physics-IQ to remove this incentive in expectation, needing only two independent samples per condition. Two-step sampling lowers Kernel Physics-IQ in all five image-to-video models. For CogVideoX, the official score rises by 0.055 while ours falls by 0.131. Changing the sampling schedule reveals further differences official scores miss. In controlled physical systems with known outcome probabilities, our metric favors the true distribution over collapsed predictions. In short, a world model should be judged not only by how closely one video matches what happened, but by how faithfully its predictions represent what could have happened.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image color enhancement aims to improve visual appeal, but previous methods often learn to reproduce ground-truth expert targets. These targets reflect the preferences of a few experts and cover a limited range of aesthetic choices. Matching them does not ensure stronger aesthetic quality across different tastes and scenes. By contrast, large generative models learn rich visual knowledge from large image collections and often produce vivid, appealing image edits. However, direct generation can change the source image's semantic structure along with its colors. In this paper, we introduce AesDistill, a framework for distilling generative aesthetics into image color enhancement. We use 4 leading open-source large generative models to produce 64,644 edited images as teacher targets at a cost of about 3,360 H100 GPU-hours, forming the AesDistill Dataset. AesDistill also includes AesDistill Student, a 1.13M-parameter model that learns teacher color changes through confidence-weighted supervision while preserving the source image's semantic structure. We evaluate AesDistill with 12 aesthetic and 4 image-quality assessment models. Every teacher's targets rank above the FiveK and PPR10K expert target means in the joint assessment. Across both datasets, AesDistill Student outperforms all 20 baseline methods and ranks directly behind the 4 teachers. These results demonstrate that distilling aesthetics from generative models enables a compact student to outperform methods trained on expert targets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): An image signal processor does more than reconstruct an image. It also has to decide how strongly to denoise, how to balance color, and which tone curve maps the scene onto the display, and these settings depend on the scene itself, the curve that suits a backlit sky is not the one that suits an indoor portrait. Most neural ISPs learn a single fixed transformation and apply it to every scene. We instead make the adaptation an explicit part of the network. Our model, LG FiLM, keeps one shared encoder decoder and places a small mixture of affine experts at its bottleneck; the experts scales, shifts, and mixing weights are predicted from local neighborhood evidence together with a global scene descriptor. Reconstruction stays in a single shared pathway and only the processing mode is switched. We evaluate LG FiLM on three datasets, Fujifilm UltraISP, Zurich RAW to RGB, and a COCO derived benchmark built with an inverse ISP. The model reaches the best reconstruction fidelity of the compared entries on the primary benchmark and transfers to the other two.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Given an image and successive user instructions, sequential image editing should return an updated image that satisfies each new request and preserves earlier requirements that remain active. This lets users develop a result without repeatedly restating or repairing their decisions. The difficulty is that a retrieved image can contain a valid color on an obsolete surface, while the current image may lack the newly requested material. Neither reference selection nor a precise object mask alone specifies how to reconcile attributes occupying the same region. We present TRACE, which separates the continuing attribute from its visual carrier and prioritizes local appearance operations under complete-request checks. Active-goal binding identifies what should persist, carrier selection supplies suitable current or newly generated pixels, and tonal calibration reduces tinting near highlights and shadows. Experiments across frozen editors, independent visual readers, new source images, and complete sequential benchmarks support joint color-material recovery and show how local execution preserves the surrounding scene.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal sarcasm detection requires textual, visual, and contextual evidence, particularly when multiple images provide complementary cues. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate auxiliary information from a post's text and images, but their outputs may mix useful background knowledge with redundant visual descriptions. We propose SEAF, a dual-branch framework for selective semantic enrichment and visual evidence fusion. SEAF assigns generated fields to distinct roles: entity background enriches semantic input, while image descriptions supply alignment statistics. The semantic branch combines the original post text, background from a frozen MLLM, and optical character recognition (OCR) text. The aligned visual branch combines global and local visual features with statistics of cross-modal and inter-image similarity. Text-Anchored Smoothed Aggregation (TASA) weights images by their similarity to the original post text while preserving contributions from images with low literal similarity. Abstract (英文): Given past images and known actions, visual continuation generates future frames from one evolving scene state. Keeping this state revisable matters when an object reappears or a later view changes an earlier interpretation. Once past images cannot be replayed, a summary sufficient for current rendering can lose candidates needed after correction. We establish matching sparse and dense bounds on the bits needed for later correction under stable observations and bounded disagreement. For static streams at the stated smoothness and margin scales, correction incurs at least logarithmic overhead in one dimension and polynomial overhead in higher dimensions. Coherent Archive Memory (CAM) stores geometric summaries with exact updates under clipped expansion and coordinate assignments, then uses historical existence constraints to select one state for all requested frames. Exhaustive photographic tests show strict worst-truth image-risk gains from joint witnesses, while controlled neural continuations recover positions hidden in the current view.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We propose VC-VQA, a large-scale benchmark for vision-centric knowledge reasoning, aiming to systematically evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition and complex multi-stage reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily treat visual information as entity recognition and rely on text-based external knowledge, VC-VQA explicitly models the role of visual information by considering both its source (internal vs. external images) and stage of involvement (pre-retrieval vs. post-retrieval). Specifically, VC-VQA requires models to retrieve external visual evidence, identify visual clues before retrieval, and localize, verify, or match retrieved knowledge against the internal image. We further construct a controlled multimodal knowledge base with traceable textual and visual evidence for each sample. We evaluate 21 representative MLLMs under four settings: no external knowledge, with provided evidence, knowledge base retrieval, and open-web search. Experimental results lead to three key findings: current models still struggle with vision-centric knowledge reasoning; fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition remains the primary bottleneck; and agentic retrieval highlights the value of multi-step planning and cross-modal knowledge integration. Overall, VC-VQA provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating vision-centric knowledge reasoning in MLLMs. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/CASIA-IVA-Lab/VC-VQA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images and videos, but every sampling step requires a full network evaluation. Training-free caching reduces this cost by reusing or forecasting network outputs across steps, but common approaches use per-model calibrated skip criteria or maintain per-layer prediction histories. We present D3-Cache, which applies Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to Diffusion sampling: it fits DMD to a short history of exact velocities and forecasts only the velocity that the scheduler consumes. A horizon-aware gate decides when to skip: whenever the network is evaluated, its output is compared with the forecast for that step, and the larger the error, the sooner the network is evaluated again, up to a cap on consecutive skipped steps. D3-Cache requires no training or per-model offline calibration curve and adds negligible memory. It shares one history length and risk tolerance across the four evaluated models. On four state-of-the-art image and video models, D3-Cache achieves speedups of 2.6-3.8x with high fidelity to the base model output.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal image fusion traditionally generates a single fused image from paired observations at a specific viewpoint, limiting comprehensive scene perception and adaptability to diverse perceptual objectives. To overcome these limitations, we present LIFT, a novel framework for our proposed language-controlled 3D image fusion (L3D-IF) paradigm that extends multimodal image fusion from 2D images to free-viewpoint 3D scenes, enabling language-guided fusion tailored to diverse perceptual objectives. First, LIFT formulates Semantic Modality Binding (SMB), which interprets language instructions to identify semantic targets and their associated modality preferences and grounds these targets in corresponding regions of a shared Gaussian scene, establishing persistent semantic-to-spatial correspondences for consistent fusion control across viewpoints. Second, Language-Conditioned Gaussian Appearance (LCGA) is developed to achieve scene-wide multimodal appearance modeling by combining adaptive multimodal blending with language- and viewpoint-conditioned appearance refinement, enabling spatially adaptive realization of diverse fusion appearances while preserving source fidelity at pure-modality endpoints. Finally, we construct a Multi-View Language-Supervision (MVLS) dataset that associates language instructions and calibrated viewpoints with modulated fused images, providing effective supervision for learning instruction-dependent fusion from finite multi-view observations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LIFT achieves coherent multimodal fusion across continuous viewpoints, accurate semantic target localization, and consistent regional fusion control under diverse language instructions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Image-to-slide reconstruction must recover both visual appearance and native object structure for subsequent editing. Yet visually faithful charts and tables may lack editable series and cells, and direct code generation couples content prediction to rendering implementation. We present Slide2PPT, a model-agnostic, training-free framework that equips both open- and closed-source vision-language models with native PowerPoint reconstruction capabilities. Through a unified typed interface, the model predicts object records while a fixed renderer materializes their native properties, enabling integration with different backbones without task-specific training or architectural changes. Localization-guided refinement then connects visual discrepancies to object records for correction. Regional updates preserve unselected records, while whole-slide updates address cross-region relationships. Record refinement and targeted object editing support structural and attribute-level changes, with rendered feedback governing iterative acceptance. To evaluate this framework across diverse reconstruction demands, we introduce Slide2PPT-Bench, covering controlled elements, real slides, and scientific figures. Evaluation separately measures file production, rendered agreement, and native-object availability. Experiments across open- and closed-source backbones demonstrate substantially more reliable PPTX generation than direct code generation, improved layout and text recovery on scientific figures, and native chart and table interfaces beyond geometric primitives.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We enable accurate low-bit recovery from small synthetic pools by improving both image synthesis and student preparation. A grid with lower local response error can incur greater error in the student's block context. Functional Grid Calibration (FGC) selects activation scales and integer offsets against full-precision responses constructed from the student's current block input. With weights fixed during calibration, FGC evaluates candidates only at their consuming operators, bringing block context to calibration at nearly local-scoring cost. For DeiT and ViT, progressive inversion updates the same images through increasingly deep teacher prefixes. With 1,000 images, the complete pipeline exceeds MimiQ's reported accuracy in all 18 W4A4/W5A5 settings across nine backbones. With 256 images, DeiT-S and ViT-S reach 68.20% and 66.90% ImageNet top-1 accuracy at W4A4 in 26-27 minutes on one RTX 5090. The pipeline outperforms strengthened 1K-image MimiQ by 13.03 and 24.36 percentage points at lower preparation cost and the same recovery-update count. The same low-budget checkpoints retain their advantage on ImageNetV2.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Human proficiency estimation is challenging because skill is a high-level and often subjective property that is not captured by action identity alone. Existing approaches leverage multimodal observations such as ego-exo video, gaze, pose, and inertial signals, but the gap between sensor-level evidence and abstract proficiency labels remains difficult to bridge. We present SkillLens, a proficiency estimation framework that uses atomic action descriptions as temporal guidance, rather than direct semantic supervision, to localize proficiency-relevant windows for dense visual-dynamics extraction. Each window is summarized into an action-dynamics feature and scored by a high-low prototype margin against higher- and lower-proficiency executions from the same activity-action category. Fusing these ego/exo PDM cues with a global ego-exo-gaze baseline through CORAL ordinal calibration, our method achieves 0.542 accuracy on Ego-Exo4D. Our project page is available at https://skill-lens.net/

Abstract (英文): DeepStack-style vision-language models feed visual tokens to the language model at its input, and again at deeper layers. We call these the main entry and the later entries. What do the later entries add when the model already has the image? We give the entries versions that differ in one object's color and see which one the answer follows. On photographs, the original DeepStack follows the later entries, while Qwen3-VL-8B/32B follow the main entry. Does weakening the main entry give the later features more influence? On synthetic images, we keep this conflict and reduce color saturation at the main entry only. The unchanged later features gain a stronger effect on the relative scores of the two colors, in all four models, including Granite Vision. This holds even under mild desaturation, when accuracy without the conflict stays at 100%. To test whether later features can also correct errors, we degrade both entries and restore only the later ones. The same blur costs both Qwen models 50 percentage points of accuracy on stripe orientation. The 32B model recovers 48 points and the 8B model only 8. Accuracy with one clean entry predicted which entry each model can lose, and five of six tested predictions held on new GQA attribute questions. With the other entry zeroed, Qwen3-VL-8B loses only 0.3 points on its main entry alone, and DeepStack at most 3.45 points on either entry alone. In split inference on these questions, DeepStack can then send only its main-entry features at higher precision. This takes 49% fewer bytes than all entries at a shared precision, at similar accuracy.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World action models (WAMs) predict future videos and actions with one backbone, and their imagined future is often seen as a safety signal. We introduce SteerTrace, a targeted universal attack on WAMs that also traces its effect inside the model. One $\ell_\infty$-bounded (4/255) perturbation per model, shared across frames, tasks, and camera views, pushes the executed action toward an attacker-chosen target. On eight WAMs from four families (FastWAM, Faster-WAM, OpenWAM and LingBot-VA) on LIBERO and RoboTwin, it drives task success close to zero and moves the attacked action toward the target. Same-budget noise and BadWAM produce little action shift in the target direction. We use the signed targeted effect to measure how much of the effect each intervention removes or reproduces. Under the standard attack, restoring future tokens removes 61-94% of the targeted effect in the five models whose action experts attend to them. When the attack is constrained to keep the predicted future close to the clean prediction, the targeted effect can become more dependent on current-frame tokens. In three of these five models, restoring current-frame tokens removes 87-99% of the effect, compared with 9-15% for future tokens. In six models, restoring just 0.36% of the FFN neurons in layers 10-29 of the video expert removes 72-96% of the targeted effect. Residual directions 2-16 hold only 11-18% of the shift energy, yet removing them cancels most of the targeted effect.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D visual grounding (3D-VG) is a task that bridges the natural language and 3D space, serving as a foundational capability for embodied intelligence and human-computer interaction. Although recent 3D-VG agents overcome the reliance on pre-processed 3D point clouds, they essentially employ a unidirectional, pipeline-style tool-calling architecture. This architecture separates semantic understanding from geometric reconstruction, leading to error accumulation and an inability to autonomously recover from failures. To address this flaw, this paper proposes the Dual-Anchor Collaborative Agent (DACA), which reinterprets 3D-VG as a semantic-geometric dual-anchor collaborative reasoning paradigm. DACA comprises two complementary specialized tools: the semantic anchor tool holds the visual semantic features of the target and achieves efficient target tracking via optical flow and VLM local verification; the geometric anchor tool holds the 3D spatial geometric features of the target and performs global spatial reasoning via voxelization and multi-keypoint projection. The two components exchange information bidirectionally via a standardized tool call protocol, autonomously deciding on the next perception-action step to form a closed-loop optimization. Experiments on the ScanRefer and Nr3D benchmarks demonstrate that zero-shot DACA achieves an overall Acc@0.25 of 73.6\% on ScanRefer and 70.4\% overall accuracy on Nr3D, far surpassing the latest zero-shot 3D-VG methods. The code and models are available at https://github.com/liujia99/DACA

</details>

Abstract (英文): Infrared small target detection is essential for early warning, surveillance, remote sensing, and space-based target perception. However, infrared small targets are typically tiny, weakly textured, and boundary-blurred, making them difficult to distinguish from complex backgrounds. Moreover, target-like background structures can produce salient responses across multiple scales, making response strength alone insufficient for reliable target discrimination. To address this challenge, we propose VBE-UNet, a Target Evidence Chain Verification Network that assesses candidate reliability beyond response magnitude through complementary evidence cues. Specifically, counterfactual background explanation models local target evidence, while joint evidence verification integrates cross-scale support and perturbation-based contribution verification to guide feature recomposition. The resulting reliability estimates guide selective feature refinement, promoting target-relevant responses while reducing unsupported background activations. Extensive experiments on IRSTD-1K, NUDT-SIRST, and NUAA-SIRST demonstrate that VBE-UNet achieves superior detection accuracy and lower false-alarm rates compared with state-of-the-art methods. Ablation studies and evidence reliability analysis further validate the effectiveness of the proposed verification framework.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Can we reveal what is inside an opaque box by observing its surface vibrations? Objects inside a box alter its mechanical response, providing cues about their hidden locations. We introduce a method that uses these cues to predict a box's 2D occupancy layout entirely remotely. A nearby speaker excites vibrations, which we measure on one visible wall using a grid of laser points and a camera measuring each point's speckle-pattern shifts. Our model, WhisperBox, learns to encode these vibrations into a latent representation and decode it into an occupancy map of the box's contents. The method requires no physical contact to excite or sense vibrations. We demonstrate layout recovery across various boxes and single- and multiple-object scenes, with generalization to unseen object instances, positions, orientations, and weights, as well as changes in laser placement and excitation source. Our analysis reveals that informative frequency bands need not coincide with the box's resonances and that the learned latent space captures object placement and supports arithmetic operations. These results demonstrate the potential of remote vibration sensing to infer spatial layouts hidden from view.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skeleton graph attention for autism video analysis weights joints by content alone, giving an erratic wrist the same standing as a still one. Stereotypy, however, is defined by how unpredictably movement unfolds rather than how much of it occurs: in our cohort, every amplitude-based kinematic descriptor falls in stereotyped clips, while windowed Sample Entropy of angular velocity alone rises (Hedges' g = +1.05). We compute this irregularity as a per-joint, per-frame tensor that structurally drives computation. The proposed chaos-guided graph attention and mixture-of-experts model (CGM) couples two mechanisms. First, a learned pairwise term added to the graph-attention logit concentrates attention on irregular joints and re-ranks the remaining edge weight toward jointly irregular pairs; its unconstrained coefficient neither vanishes nor inverts, and zeroing the tensor realigns attention in 94% of clips. Second, a gate conditioned on the same signal routes each frame to two of four experts, whose weights track frame-level irregularity. Under subject-grouped five-fold cross-validation on 116 clips, CGM reaches 0.930 balanced accuracy, exceeding the graph-attention baseline by +0.187; the component ordering replicates on the public MMASD dataset, and on held-out Panoptic Studio sessions, CGM keeps 0.86 specificity without retraining, against at most 0.35 for chaos-free baselines. In ablation, routing without irregularity yields no reliable gain, whereas chaos modulation alone contributes +0.132. A severity index from the same tensor reaches 0.87 split-half reliability, separates the cohorts with Hedges' g = +1.08, and stays consistent within children across therapy recordings, supporting video-only monitoring of stereotypy during intervention.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Professional video workflows operate at qualities that learned video codecs do not reach. We measure how much of a professional quality range a codec covers, from the lowest quality of the standard test conditions, VVC at QP 37, to the highest 4:2:2 grade of the production codec ProRes. To compare inter-frame coding alone, every codec receives original frames as references at the intra positions. On 10-bit YCbCr 4:2:2 test sequences, four recent learned codecs cover 24 to 38% of this range. Their encoders are feed-forward networks with a fixed latent capacity, the number of latent values per sample, of at most 0.25, and removing their quantization raises the quality by at most 1 dB. We propose a learned inter-frame codec whose encoder searches, as the encoders of conventional codecs do. It searches block motion, which a learned network refines, and it searches among learned block transforms whose latent capacity ranges up to 1, selecting the partition, the skip decisions, and the quantized coefficients by a rate-distortion cost. Our codec reaches the upper end of the range on all six sequences. With all residual blocks fixed to 16x16, whose latent capacity is 0.25, the same codec covers 55% of the range, with or without quantization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Promptable segmenters answer what a thing is. Dense prediction tasks also ask class-agnostic questions, such as what stands out or hides, how light is blocked, transmitted or reflected, and where structure lies. Robots, surveillance systems and image editors build on their answers. We study promptable concept maps (PCM), which extend SAM 3's promptable concept segmentation from noun instances to maps of concepts named by short noun phrases such as `"transparent object", with "none" as an answer. PCM-Bench groups the standard test sets of seven such tasks by the question they ask. On it, we find that SAM 3 sees more than it detects. Its semantic path responds to many of these concepts, yet a low image-level presence score can block every detection of its instance path. We present UNVEIL, which trains a copy of SAM 3's semantic path for these maps, with LoRA on copies of its last blocks. A separate presence decoder learns from verified negatives whether a concept occurs. With one text phrase per query, UNVEIL maps region concepts such as salient and camouflaged objects, shadows, transparent objects and mirrors. The same weights map thin structures such as edges and skeletons as well as object categories. Run next to SAM 3, it leaves SAM 3's own outputs unchanged. On each of the seven tasks, UNVEIL reaches the highest score among the compared promptable models, including SAM 3's official fine-tuning and pipelines built on multimodal LLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/yuhuan-wu/UNVEIL.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Longitudinal PSMA-PET/CT registration is a key step in assessing treatment response in prostate cancer, and it requires more than generic alignment accuracy: the deformation must preserve lesion volume, so that measured changes in metabolic tumor volume (MTV) and total lesion glycolysis (TLG) reflect treatment response rather than registration artifacts. We propose AdaptReg, which adapts a frozen registration foundation model to such task-specific requirements through a lightweight trainable adapter branch with identity-preserving initialization. Keeping uniGradICON fully frozen, we apply FiLM-style per-voxel affine modulation ($γ$, $β$) to its decoder skip connections; the modulation is generated from anatomical semantic features of a frozen TotalSegmentator encoder and supervised by a lesion volume-preservation loss. On the official Codabench validation set (20 pairs), AdaptReg achieves DSC 0.7585, HD95 6.33 mm, MTV/TLG errors of 1.68%/2.11%, and 0.0022% non-diffeomorphic voxels, improving on the uniGradICON + instance optimization baseline by $+0.05$ DSC and reducing the MTV/TLG errors by 70.9%/67.7%. On the hidden official test set (160 pairs), AdaptReg achieves CT DSC 0.816, PET DSC 0.598, and MTV/TLG errors of 4.02%/3.46%, ranking fourth among 16 teams in the final PSMAReg evaluation. Abstract (英文): Identifying a driver's gaze target provides object-level information for visual attention monitoring in intelligent vehicles. Gaze-zone classification and continuous attention localization provide limited information about the identity of the attended scene target. This paper proposes a modular framework that identifies object- or region-level gaze targets from synchronized in-cabin driver images and forward-view road images. The framework comprises four components: MFGaze, which combines a Vision Transformer pretrained with a masked autoencoder (MAE) and head-pose conditioning through feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM); panoptic scene understanding, which constructs a structured candidate set; polynomial ridge regression, which maps gaze features to 2D scene-image coordinates through individualized calibration; and entropy-driven target inference, which adaptively fuses boundary-distance geometry with class-level semantic priors. Experiments evaluate the framework using three public gaze datasets, a laboratory test using Cityscapes road scenes and self-collected gaze data, and the Look Both Ways (LBW) dataset. The complete framework achieves an overall Top-1 gaze target identification accuracy of 96.27% in the laboratory experiment. After sequence-specific calibration, it achieves Top-1 accuracies of 79.92% for task-relevant objects and 85.58% for the road class on LBW. MFGaze achieves the lowest mean angular errors among the compared methods, with 4.06$^\circ$, 4.23$^\circ$, and 10.52$^\circ$ on MPIIFaceGaze, ETH-XGaze, and Gaze360, respectively, and demonstrates strong cross-dataset generalization. Sensitivity analyses show stable performance under hyperparameter perturbations. These results support the framework as a source of object-level gaze information for driver monitoring.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the stage in which Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) advance their capability to reason over video. However, the VideoLLM also learns to answer from textual regularities of its training data, a shortcut that bypasses the video. The regularities behind the shortcut are hard to define at the data level, and the shortcut forms regardless of training epoch, data size, or backbone. The shortcut is hidden behind a rise in accuracy within the training distribution, yet it is not required for the rise and even costs SFT the gain the video offers under distribution shift. We therefore propose Video-Evidence Training via Orthogonal projection (VETO), which intervenes on the fine-tuning update where the shortcut is written rather than on the data. VETO calibrates the subspace that the text alone occupies in the activations of the base model and projects the update onto its orthogonal complement. The VideoLLM is thus left to ground its answer in the full input, with the shortcut suppressed as it forms and never defined. Across seven VideoLLMs, VETO preserves the gain of SFT on the training distribution and suppresses the shortcut. On six benchmarks under shift it gains more than SFT and answers from the full input rather than the shortcut. Code is available at https://github.com/Youngwoo-git/VETO.

</details>

Abstract (英文): GeoPillars is a pillar-based 3D detector for single-sweep LiDAR under a hard budget of eight points per 0.4 m pillar, a budget that discards 46.6% of in-range points. It adds geometric pillar features, grouped vector attention within each pillar, a learned absolute positional prior on the bird's-eye-view (BEV) grid, a large-kernel backbone, auxiliary surface-normal supervision and a full-resolution detection head. Trained on 123k synthetic CARLA scans and evaluated on cars, it reaches 81.33 BEV AP@0.7, 13.76 points above CenterPoint at the same input budget and 1.91 points above CenterPoint at its native, denser budget. These are in-distribution figures, because the validation split is per-frame rather than scene-disjoint. A rung-by-rung decomposition shows that the new components explain little of the gain. Raising the detection-head resolution alone recovers 10.19 of the 13.76 points, the joint change of encoder, backbone, neck and positional prior adds 3.37, and the normal-supervision branch adds 0.20, which is within run-to-run variation. With heading reversals folded out, orientation error is similar for all seven models, although GeoPillars reverses headings about half as often as a resolution-matched baseline. It is also the slowest of the seven models. Evaluated zero-shot on 6,743 real nuScenes keyframes, no model transfers, and each retains 3.7% to 12.2% of its CARLA accuracy. A collection defect left LiDAR intensity constant, so every model learned from geometry alone. Code and checkpoints are publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present FAB (Fast and Accurate Body estimation), a human body estimator that jointly improves inference efficiency and body-recovery fidelity. Rather than predicting only parametric mesh-model parameters, FAB directly predicts human skeleton joints and dense surface points. In images, it predicts dense 2D and 3D body points alongside a parametric Momentum Human Rig (MHR) head, then fits SMPL in one learned pass followed by two Gauss-Newton steps. Its ViT-L student, FAB-L, distilled from a fine-tuned SAM 3D Body, runs in real time for one person on an RTX 4070 Ti SUPER, from detection to a fitted body, at 4.6x the speed of SAM 3D Body's eager-PyTorch body-only path, while improving accuracy on most camera-space benchmarks. The same network predicts heel, toe, hand, and knee contacts, which connect the body to the scene. FAB also estimates world-space motion, fuses static-scene depth around the visible person, and aligns body and scene using predicted contacts. For human-to-humanoid real-to-sim transfer, an in-model G1 head and FAB-Retarget, a motion-level retargeter that reads dense points or the fitted mesh, map motion to a Unitree G1. FAB-Retarget produces references that follow the person more closely than specialized retargeters including SOMA, GMR, UMR, and OmniRetarget, and substantially more closely than Fast SAM 3D Body retargeted with our sequence IK. Project page: https://www.jinkuncao.com/FAB.

Abstract (英文): Existing 3D generation methods synthesize visually compelling scenes from fixed inputs, yet struggle to continually expand and revise a 3D world as new observations arrive. Iterative approaches often fuse real observations and generated content into a single representation, treating generated content as established structure rather than revisable predictions. Consequently, early generation errors can persist and accumulate despite new evidence. We propose World-delta, an incremental 3D scene generation framework combining uncertainty-aware persistent world modeling with delta-guided diffusion for continual scene expansion and correction. Our framework comprises three components: 1) Visual Evidence Encoding integrates geometric and semantic cues from incoming images into unified world tokens. 2) Observation-Calibrated World Modeling maintains a persistent latent world state that distinguishes observation-supported content from provisional completion hypotheses. New evidence drives selective world deltas to revise conflicting hypotheses while preserving well-supported content. 3) Incremental World Diffusion Generation uses delta-derived masks to restrict diffusion to regions requiring updates, freezing unchanged regions. Only affected 3D chunks are decoded and integrated into the existing scene. Together, these components enable continual world expansion and revision while reducing redundant generation and preserving scene consistency. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of World-delta in incremental 3D scene generation, observation-driven correction, scene consistency, and update efficiency over successive scene updates.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Preference training is the standard recipe for reducing object hallucination in vision-language models, and each new method reports a lower CHAIR score. We ask what that reduction consists of. Every trained system is compared with its base model's caption truncated to the same object recall, which removes the part of the gain that comes from saying less; the remainder is compared with what a deliberately weak, frozen, eleven-feature post-hoc probe fitted on 500 annotated images can reproduce. All criteria were specified in the analysis code and frozen before the outputs they judge existed. For eight public methods on LLaVA-1.5-7B, the probe reproduces at least two thirds of what training removes beyond saying less for 7 of 8 methods on COCO, 8 of 8 on AMBER and 6 of 8 on Object HalBench, with RLAIF-V the consistent exception; a later public method released for both LLaVA-1.5 and Qwen2.5-VL (SENTINEL) meets the same bar on both. On our own DPO checkpoints the accounting holds on Qwen2.5-VL but fails on InternVL2.5 on COCO (2 of 4 training designs). At matched recall, the base model plus the probe beats truncation for 8 of 9 LLaVA-1.5 systems on COCO and 9 of 9 on AMBER, on the two newer backbones, and when realized as actual sentence deletion. The same measure prices training signals: DPO on the model's own samples labelled by the probe or by an object-level CLIP score recovers 0.70 and 0.87 of the beyond-brevity gain of ground-truth-labelled DPO on Qwen2.5-VL (the probe 0.73 on InternVL2.5), both beat length-matched random labels, and CLIP labels buy more than probe labels on COCO. Human judgements show that the sentences the probe deletes are wrong more often than those truncation would delete; a human check of the CHAIR evaluator's precision falls below our pre-set bar, which we report as a limitation.

Abstract (英文): Temporal action localization (TAL) detectors must identify actions and estimate their temporal boundaries, but localization remains a major source of detection errors. While understanding what determines boundary placement requires faithful attribution of temporal evidence, existing attribution methods have not sufficiently addressed the temporal structure of TAL or the need to tailor attribution to boundary localization. We introduce a structure-aware attribution framework that averages contributions across random evidence-restoration orders following the Shapley-Shubik principle. We make it tractable by using the detector's receptive field to limit which time steps are ordered individually, and by approximating each order with a soft gradient path. For boundary predictions, we further introduce cell-structured restoration orders and boundary-anchored references that selectively remove adjacent evidence. We also adapt faithfulness evaluation to assess the joint effects of contiguous temporal evidence and the direction of boundary displacement. Experiments on THUMOS14 and EPIC-KITCHENS-100 with TriDet and ActionFormer demonstrate improved attribution faithfulness over existing methods. Attribution analysis and controlled interventions further reveal that class-score changes do not reliably track boundary displacement, providing input-level evidence that classification relies on content less informative about boundary placement. Code and evaluation artifacts will be publicly released.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Industrial bin picking requires pose estimates that are accurate under clutter, partial occlusion, and the reflective, texture-less nature of manufactured parts. Most recent approaches to 6D pose estimation follow multi-stage pipelines. These achieve high accuracy, but incur memory and runtime footprints that do not cope with the operational constraints of many real-world industrial deployments. Holistic pipelines estimate poses in a single pass, with cost controlled through indiscriminate subsampling or lowered resolution. Continuing this line, we present SCCV-6D, a fully sparse multi-view RGB-D estimator. We reconstruct the 3D space at millimeter precision, prune background via a staged heatmap mechanism, and predict class, translation offset and rotation at highest resolution for an arbitrary number of objects simultaneously. Rotation is formulated as canonical coordinate voting. Points vote for their canonical object coordinates with an associated confidence, enabling closed-form pose recovery with a symmetry-aware weighted Procrustes solve. Along an in-cluster point recovery induced by offset votes, this removes time-consuming post-processing stages. We reuse the task-supervised attention priors from the heatmap formulation for a novel attention-gated distillation objective that reweights teacher-student feature alignment. On the IPD test split we report competitive 0.826 AP at 1.13 s per scene, significantly faster than the leading entries. On MV-YCB our approach reaches 96.1 ADD-S, and remains competitive even on the densely packed bins of ROBI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We examine how well subspaces constructed from local curvature represent the change in the gradient update field after a finite step in semantic segmentation models. The local response is the action of the curvature operator on the actual input displacement. In a study of 50 images and two models, we measure the ratio of the loss increase attained in a fixed subspace to that attained in the full space. This ratio decreases as the search radius increases. On ten images from the same sample, we compare the one-dimensional subspace spanned by the initial gradient with two rank-16 subspaces for both models. The two equal-rank subspaces are assessed at the same initial states, using the same actual steps and measured vectors. Adding generalised Gauss-Newton directions to the initial gradient reduces the mean relative error in representing the field change from 0.922 to 0.874 for the first model and from 0.819 to 0.763 for the second. At the same rank, including the response direction for the initial gradient nearly eliminates the error in representing the local response. The mean error in representing the field change does not decrease. A decomposition with fixed pixel weights shows that the finite-step remainder is comparable in norm to the field change and makes the largest diagonal contribution to the squared projection error of the field after the step. Evaluation of a search subspace should measure how accurately it represents the field change and the local response.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Precise forest species mapping using spaceborne hyperspectral data is challenging due to high spectral similarity and the inherent sub-pixel heterogeneity of natural forest stands. While traditional deep learning models exploit spatial context through convolutional architectures, these approaches can introduce noise in fragmented or mixed-species forests. In this paper, we propose a hierarchy-aware, multimodal deep learning framework to resolve sub-pixel forest composition by integrating PRISMA (PRecursore IperSpettrale della Missione Applicativa) hyperspectral imagery, high-resolution panchromatic data, and digital elevation models. The architecture features a multi-branch encoder combining an EfficientNetV2-based one-dimensional convolutional neural network (CNN) for spectral signatures and twin two-dimensional CNNs for spatial and topographic features, coupled with a novel structurally-masked Transformer. By using a binary transition tensor to condition the Transformer slot embeddings and constrain inference, the model enforces taxonomic consistency between broad forest categories and fine-grained species distributions. The framework is validated across three diverse Italian biogeographical regions (the Alps and the Central and Southern Apennines) using field-surveyed plots. The proposed method consistently outperforms state-of-the-art multi-label and ranking-based paradigms, particularly in identifying subordinate species and their relative dominance. The bidirectional learning mechanism allows sub-pixel compositional features to refine macroscopic forest type predictions, while the integration of topographic priors effectively resolves spectral ambiguities in complex terrains. This knowledge-driven method supports regional-scale biodiversity monitoring and forest characterization within the evaluated PRISMA scenarios.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Spurious correlations can give image predictors high in-distribution accuracy but poor generalization under distribution shift. We introduce $\textbf{Rank2Replace}$, which transfers knowledge of task-relevant evidence from pretrained vision--language models (VLMs) to small predictors through training-time augmentation for out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization. The VLM ranks object-centric slots by task relevance and guides rank-dependent replacement with same-rank slot embeddings from another image. This augmentation retains the original image's label, encouraging reliance on preserved task evidence as incidental cues change, and supports combination with existing OOD methods. Across four datasets and six splits under shared frozen slot embeddings, the default cross-entropy configuration reaches macro-averaged average-group and worst-group accuracies of $80.43\%$ and $65.28\%$ without environment labels or dataset-specific replacement tuning. Slot corruption improves both macro-averaged metrics for all fourteen OOD training methods. Combining it with slot ranking and IRM reaches $81.96\%$ and $72.68\%$. Controlled ablations support the ranking and replacement design, with gains across five ranking VLMs and six extractor--predictor combinations. Permutation-invariant predictors further improve both macro-averaged metrics over their unaugmented counterparts while using VLM guidance only during training for efficient inference.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Interactive game world models separate executable world evolution from visual generation, using video models to render engine-provided scene representations into detailed observations. During sustained gameplay, however, existing generative renderers struggle to follow changing conditions while preserving visual continuity across diverse proxies and target styles. We therefore introduce ContextRenderer, an autoregressive video diffusion framework designed for both dynamic conditioning and cross-proxy generalization. It couples Dynamic-Context Learning (DCL) for chunk-level interaction supervision with Recurrent History Compression (RHC) to follow condition updates while preserving visual continuity within a bounded token budget. Across proxy representations, Cross-Representation Flow Alignment (CRFA) connects proxy reconstruction with appearance and interaction variation through a shared high-fidelity reconstruction. WorldPrism, our interaction-centric dataset constructed from agent-built worlds, AAA gameplay, and open-source videos, supplies the corresponding renderings and chunk-level annotations for these learning tasks. Experiments on GWM-Bench and Solar-Bench show improved reconstruction and visual quality, while qualitative results demonstrate minute-long interactive rendering under changing prompts and subject references. Project page: https://rainzor.github.io/contextrenderer/

Abstract (英文): Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Flow matching enables high-quality image and video generation, but its iterative sampling process requires repeated evaluations of large neural networks. Existing few-step methods can distort the output and reduce its agreement with a many-step reference. We propose TrajCal, a training-free calibration framework for few-step sampling. Based on scatter search, a well-established metaheuristic for difficult optimization problems, TrajCal starts with diverse seed schedules, deforms and combines these candidates, and selects one fixed schedule by final output fidelity for reuse on new inputs. For both image and video generation, TrajCal achieves higher reference fidelity than all compared baselines at low evaluation budgets while maintaining competitive visual quality. The search is effective even with randomly generated seeds, while trajectory-based seeds further improve fidelity. For cross-style generalization, photography-calibrated schedules also improve fidelity on unseen image styles.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recalling where and when an object was last seen and who saw it requires recognizing it across days and people. Existing long-video benchmarks focus on events, object categories, or relative locations, leaving instance-level spatial memory largely untested in multimodal models. To address this gap, we present EgoWitness, a benchmark for instance-level spatial memory and reasoning in egocentric video. Its 8,453 human-verified questions span approximately 324 hours of week-long and short-horizon recordings and test seven abilities, from recognizing an object or room to recalling who saw it and reasoning about room visits. Over one million reviewed masks of 34 registered landmarks and 21 movable-object labels are linked through room timelines. Among twelve direct vision-language models (VLMs), open-weight models reach at most 46.97% accuracy, and none exceeds 35% on identifying who saw an object last. We therefore propose EgoClue, a spatial-memory agent that retrieves candidate intervals, verifies instance identity, and computes spatial and temporal relations with five tools. Two-stage training with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) raises its 4B controller from 23.64% to 72.65% accuracy on 1,320 held-out questions. Evidence-trace synthesis matches the best direct VLM at 74.85% on week-long questions and achieves 80.56% in environments excluded from training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recognizing when a requested spatial condition holds in streaming video can require maintaining and updating spatial knowledge across changing viewpoints. We study this capability through proactive spatial reasoning, where a model receives a query in advance and determines whether and when to respond as the stream unfolds. We introduce ProSpace-Bench, comprising nine tasks across object, camera, and scene reasoning, and ProSpace-Train for supervised training. To support spatial decisions, we propose ProSpace-VLM, which injects explicit geometric encodings estimated causally from incoming frames into intermediate VLM layers, using the existing KV cache as spatial memory and spatial token selection for efficient streaming. Experiments reveal a substantial gap between VLMs and humans, while ProSpace-VLM improves timely responses and correct abstention over fine-tuned baselines. Analyses examine how ProSpace-VLM represents previously observed spatial information across changing viewpoints.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurately extracting and mapping road surface elements is crucial for applications such as high-definition maps, road asset management, and intelligent transportation systems. This study first provides a comprehensive review of the benchmark datasets for extracting and mapping road elements, encompassing overhead-to-perspective data, large-to-local scales, and coarse-to-fine details. Subsequently, we thoroughly review the relevant approaches for extracting road surface elements. However, we argue that existing datasets still require enhancements in semantic richness, data modality diversity, and scenario complexity. To bridge these gaps, this paper presents WHU-Road3D, a large-scale multimodal dataset for fine-grained road surface mapping to address these shortcomings. WHU-Road3D provides approximately 100 km of diverse Chinese highways and urban streets. It features a rich combination of MLS point clouds, associated panoramic images, vehicle trajectories, detailed vectorized annotations, and pointwise instance annotations for road surface elements. The dataset carefully annotates 15 distinct categories of road elements, including common markings like road boundaries, zebra crossings, lane dividers, stop lines, as well as 10 types of direction symbols. To our knowledge, WHU-Road3D stands as the first multimodal dataset for mapping such a comprehensive range of road surface elements, designed to facilitate cutting-edge research in instance segmentation, full-element extraction and mapping on road surfaces. Beyond these immediate applications, WHU-Road3D also provides a valuable source for hierarchical road topology reasoning, map updating, and street scene generation. The dataset, along with the code for our benchmark methods, will be made publicly available at \href{https://github.com/WHU-USI3DV/WHU-Road3D.git}{https://github.com/WHU-USI3DV/WHU-Road3D.git}.

Abstract (英文): Although memory-augmented architectures have expanded the ability of large vision-language models (LVLMs) to process long videos, existing retrieval processes still largely rely on global semantic matching and lack state-driven temporal control, often returning fragmented or redundant evidence and failing to maintain a coherent evidence chain. To address this challenge, we propose EvoMem, a time-aware long-video memory agent guided by self-evolving retrieval skills. EvoMem constructs a hierarchical time-indexed memory, where different types of evidence are aligned onto a unified video timeline; during retrieval, temporal cues from acquired evidence are further converted into explicit signals that constrain subsequent memory access. To address recurring procedural errors in long-video question answering, such as unclear retrieval targets, improper memory access paths, or premature stopping, EvoMem introduces retrieval skills to provide process-level guidance, enabling the agent to dynamically adjust retrieval content, memory operations, and target temporal ranges according to the current evidence state, while deciding when to stop. Furthermore, inspired by reinforcement learning, we formulate skill evolution as a reward-guided policy improvement process. Each candidate skill is treated as an external policy patch, and rewards are assigned according to answer-correctness state transitions on the validation set. In this way, effective skills are accepted and low-quality skills are rejected without updating model parameters. Experiments on four long-video question answering benchmarks show that EvoMem significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average accuracy of 60.1% and an average improvement of 8.6 percentage points over the previous best methods, demonstrating its effectiveness for long-video reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-supervised foundation models are often said to need fewer labels than supervised training. We test this on a forestry task: classifying 20 tree species classes in 0.2 m aerial images from the TreeSatAI dataset. We compare a frozen DINOv3 model pretrained on 493 million satellite images, with only a linear classifier trained on top, against an ImageNet ResNet-50 that is fine-tuned end to end. On the official split, fine-tuning wins clearly (macro F1 0.764 vs. 0.685). However, 28.8% of the official test patches overlap a training or validation patch, and copying the label of the nearest such patch is correct for 83.6% of them. We therefore build a spatial split that holds out whole 2 km squares with a 200 m buffer. On this split the comparison becomes inconclusive (0.648 vs. 0.640, paired 95% CI of the difference [-0.054, 0.016]), and the learning curves of the two models overlap at every label budget from 343 to 33,126 training patches. On the five rarest classes, the frozen model achieves a higher mean F1 (0.566 vs. 0.445), although the confidence interval for this difference includes zero ([-0.05, 0.23]). Diagnostics show that the frozen features mainly miss differences between closely related species, such as the two native oaks. Choosing the input size and layer on validation data (224 px, block 16) raises the frozen model to 0.662 on the spatial split. A comparison that looks decisive on the official split is thus inconclusive under geographically separated evaluation. We release the spatial split and our code.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision Language Models (VLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in qualitative spatial reasoning, but remain limited in quantitative spatial reasoning. Current work leverages the VLM agent to invoke external vision tools to perform computation. However, these methods usually bind each functional requirement to a single predefined vision specialist, implicitly resulting in fixed and rigid tool pipelines. In practice, addressing diverse spatial scenarios and tasks demands a broader diversity in pipeline orchestration and vision specialist selection to achieve robust spatial reasoning. However, such diversity leads to a combinatorial execution space, making it challenging for the agent to orchestrate the optimal pipelines and specialists for different scenarios.
  In this work, we propose SpatialMem, a memory framework that enables a VLM agent to accumulate tool-use experience and leverage that memory to orchestrate vision specialists to solve the spatial reasoning problem. SpatialMem builds a two-level memory structure, with task memory capturing tool orchestration and scene memory capturing tool selection. It further introduces a dual-state trajectory optimization process to preserve successful tool experiences and refine suboptimal ones. Moreover, existing spatial reasoning benchmarks suffer from quality issues and limited coverage of scene types and tasks. To address these gap, we propose MetricBench, a benchmark covering both indoor and outdoor scenes and three quantitative spatial reasoning tasks. Our method achieves state-of-the-art results on MetricBench, Q-Spatial, and SpatilRGPT-Bench, outperforming best VLM baselines by 32% on MetricBench. Additionally, the memory design yields a 9.1% absolute gain over a memory-free agent on MetricBench.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Flat color-block drafts specify layout and regional color while leaving interior detail unspecified, yet pretrained flow-based generators can preserve their flat appearance even under photorealistic prompts. We trace this behavior to a cross-scale mechanism linking fine pixel variation to regional means in variational autoencoder (VAE) latent space. Flattening photo regions systematically shifts their latent means, with offsets concentrated along a shared channel direction within each backbone. Controlled field exchanges in FLUX.1-dev and Qwen-Image establish the effect of this displacement on texture generation: adding the photo-derived regional mean field to a flat latent restores texture, whereas adding its complementary field alone largely preserves flatness. A second-order encoding expansion explains how encoder curvature can convert zero-mean perturbations into mean shifts. An analytically solvable conditional Gaussian flow shows how regional noise averaging enables near-unit endpoint sensitivity at high signal-to-noise ratios. This mechanism motivates training-free rendering through pixel dither before encoding, mean-field scaling, and early boundary holding. Paired low-rank adaptation (LoRA) with draft conditioning improves content recovery and produces photo-like regional means under the standard velocity objective. Explicit regional mean guidance remains effective after adaptation, continuously controlling texture and improving color fidelity. These findings connect encoding shifts to persistent generation behavior and support interventions evaluated for texture, spatial fidelity, and semantic recovery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables real-time novel view synthesis, but Gaussian rasterization attenuates fine-scale scene structure through the projected covariance of each primitive. We show that contracting these projected covariances at inference time can mitigate the effective bandwidth limitation induced by finite Gaussian footprints, motivating a multi-aperture rendering framework for super-resolution.
  Based on this insight, we propose AGSR (Aperture-Guided Super-Resolution), a lightweight inference-time framework that progressively fuses multi-aperture renderings using uncertainty-guided feature aggregation and geometric conditioning from transmittance and depth cues. AGSR operates as a plug-in enhancement for pre-trained 3DGS models.
  Experiments on the Mip-NeRF 360 benchmark show that AGSR outperforms lightweight image-space super-resolution baselines, with gains increasing as the underlying Gaussian representation deteriorates. The results provide evidence that covariance contraction exposes bandwidth-sensitive structural cues attenuated by native Gaussian rasterization. AGSR achieves these improvements while maintaining real-time performance with up to 139k parameters.

</details>
