Abstract (英文): First-person manipulation datasets treat the observation-action pair as the atomic unit and compress the generation and execution of an action into a policy network's weights, rendering the intent state behind the action unobservable. Human motor control research shows that intent mediates between observation and action and leaves observable traces along the intent pathway from decision to effect: gaze reaches the target before the hand, muscle load builds before contact, and cortical activity precedes the motor command. To record this pathway at scale, we present HumanCOIN, a first-person manipulation dataset built around human intent: 12,500 hours across 38 scene classes with three tiers of proxies. At the output end, the L1 attention tier (10,000 h) infers gaze from headband camera video, exposing the operator's next object of attention and reach before the hand moves. Midway, the L2 exertion tier (2,000 h) records surface EMG from armbands, making load expectations readable before execution is complete. At the origin, the L3 cognition tier (500 h) records scalp EEG, giving early readouts of task decisions and monitoring. Each tier inherits all proxies from the tiers below. The dataset further provides multi-view RGB images, depth maps and point clouds, 6-DoF head and wrist pose, and step-level action and object annotations. The controlled L3 setting additionally measures gaze, hand pose and finger kinematics directly. Clock and calibration data are released with the dataset so that any read rate can be re-aligned. The three tiers cover three stations, from visual orienting to cortical preparation, making the process behind visual-action pairs available for modeling at dataset scale. Project page: https://flagopen.github.io/HumanCOIN.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Retrieval-augmented vision-language-action (VLA) models enable efficient test-time adaptation by leveraging expert demonstrations as additional context without updating model parameters. However, even when retrieved demonstrations are relevant to the current task, the VLA may fail to effectively utilize the retrieved information, ultimately leading to task failure. To address this challenge, we propose R2-VLA, a risk-controlled retrieval-augmented VLA framework that enables reliable test-time adaptation by satisfying a user-specified task success rate requirement with minimal expert assistance. Specifically, R2-VLA learns an uncertainty predictor that estimates the uncertainty of the VLA's predicted actions given the retrieved demonstrations, guiding when to act autonomously or request expert assistance. We further introduce a task-level risk-control procedure that calibrates the uncertainty threshold to satisfy a user-specified task-failure risk requirement. Moreover, R2-VLA continually incorporates expert corrections into its retrieval memory to improve future adaptation while preserving the prescribed task-level risk guarantee. Experiments across three VLA backbones and four LIBERO benchmark suites demonstrate that R2-VLA improves average task success by 9.2--11.2 percentage points, achieving the desired target with fewer expert requests. These results highlight the effectiveness of connecting the usefulness of retrieved demonstrations with task-level risk control to enable successful robotic manipulation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Recent advances in image restoration have greatly improved global image fidelity, producing visually plausible results across most regions of an image. Nevertheless, even high-quality restorations often exhibit localized failures, including artifacts in semantically important regions such as faces and text, structural inconsistencies, and texture mismatches. Although these errors typically affect only a small portion of the image, they often dominate the overall perceptual quality of the result. In this work, we propose Failure-Aware Image Restoration (FAIR) to address the "last-mile" problem in image restoration. Rather than applying one uniform generative prior over the whole image, we condition a single-pass restoration model on both where local failures are likely to occur and what corrective guidance each region requires. This region-bound conditioning enables fine-grained and controllable restoration that improves semantic accuracy and stylistic consistency in critical regions while maintaining faithful, high-quality restoration elsewhere. Our method complements existing restoration pipelines by shifting the emphasis from global fidelity to local perceptual acceptability. Extensive experiments show that the proposed approach resolves common failure cases and improves perceptual quality, bridging the gap between technical accuracy and practical usability.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Proactive video assistants must decide not only what to say, but also when to speak. Existing reinforcement-learning objectives can reward timely, correct responses without accounting for response volume, thereby encouraging unnecessary or repeated emissions. We identify this failure mode in MMDuet2, whose proactive area-under-the-curve (PAUC) reward promotes timely responses but does not price the number of emissions. We show analytically that this makes additional responses weakly penalized or effectively free, systematically encouraging over-emission. We then propose an emission-aware F1 reward for interval-valued annotations, using exclusive span--emission matching to price false emissions and missed spans. Under MMDuet2's training recipe, our policy on the visual subset of OmniPro replies 2.5x less often than MMDuet2 while raising timing F1 from 0.259 to 0.396 and content-aware F1 from 0.103 to 0.154; a more conservative variant cuts replies by 82.5% and raises precision from 0.161 to 0.418. On ProactiveVideoQA, the same reward adds replies where the baseline is nearly silent, and PAUC ranks a heavily over-emitting policy second despite near-last F1, because it is insensitive to response count. Our results indicate that proactive assistants should be trained and evaluated with objectives that price emissions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Feed-forward models have been very successful in 3D reconstruction with ad hoc attention designs that relate all view patches densely. However, the cost of this dense global attention grows quadratically with the number of views, preventing the model from applying to larger-scale reconstruction settings. In this paper, we argue that most dense attention computations are unnecessary from a classical structure-from-motion perspective: 3D structures stem from geometrically matching regions in 2D. We bring this principle to learning-based feed-forward reconstruction with our Sparse Geometric Attention Reconstruction (SGAR) method. In SGAR, each patch token attends to only a small set of patch tokens from other views, chosen by a lightweight learned key selection module trained to predict cross-view 3D proximity of tokens. Thus, dense global attention is replaced by much more efficient geometric-similarity-guided sparse attention. SGAR is a general method that can be applied to different feed-forward models by fine-tuning. In our experiments, we show that SGAR is the fastest among both state-of-the-art dense and sparse methods and the most accurate sparse one. It runs up to 12-times faster than dense attention at 1,000 views with stable pose accuracy where existing sparse methods fail, and shows the best performance in reconstructing more complete geometries on the same time budget.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Native 3D generation has largely been built around latent diffusion, with recent advances concentrating on stronger variational autoencoders (VAE) and increasingly elaborate latent representations, from vecset and triplanes to structured sparse voxels. Despite their architectural differences, these methods all share the same premise: geometry is first compressed into a latent space and only then modeled by the generator. We take a different route and propose Direct3D-V3, a latent-free diffusion transformer (DiT) framework that discards the VAE altogether and operates in the native voxel domain, directly modeling raw SDF voxel grids during both training and sampling. To make this direct voxel formulation geometrically reliable, we introduce an implicit manifold regularization over neighboring voxels to suppress non-watertight surfaces, and convert predicted SDF grids into meshes via Marching Cubes so that rendered normal losses can supervise the model directly from its predicted SDF. To improve alignment between the conditioning image and generated geometry, Direct3D-V3 also predicts the camera parameters of the conditioning image as an auxiliary task, encouraging the DiT to learn the mapping from image observations to 3D shape. This latent-free design removes the reconstruction bottleneck, brings training under an objective in the native voxel domain, and yields meshes with substantially richer local detail. The predicted camera and native SDF voxel output further make Direct3D-V3 extensible to generative reconstruction, where diffusion is interleaved with normal-guided refinement to improve alignment with the observed view while the generative prior completes unseen regions. Project page: https://dreamtechai.github.io/Direct3D-V3.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Most world models for future prediction prioritize aestetic quality of generated videos, but offer only weak physical grounding and limited geometric consistency over time, due to the lack of explicit 4D modeling. In this paper, we introduce plan4d, a possible next milestone towards building a generative plannable 4D world model that predicts future RGB videos together with dense 4D geometry, including depth, cameras, point clouds, and scene flow from an observed scene context and text-specified actions. Since training data with paired actions and dense 4D annotations is inherently scarce, plan4d leverages the complementary priors of foundational video generators and 3D reconstructors. Specifically, we propose a model-stitching architecture that couples Depth Anything 3 with LongCat-Video and augments it with an auxiliary DPT head for scene-flow prediction. The resulting model allows us to plan and generate complex, long-horizon, and fine-grained actions in a dynamic 3D scenes, while substantially improving both visual fidelity and geometric accuracy of the generated future videos. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that plan4d achieves state-of-the-art performance in generative 4D modeling of environmental reactions. Abstract (英文): Reliable extraction of calcium-dependent fluorescence traces in calcium imaging sequences requires identifying pixels whose fluorescence intensity reflects the same underlying cellular activity. We present a deterministic temporal embedding framework that maps pixel-wise fluorescence traces to a low-dimensional representation in which temporally coherent sources form spatially coherent regions. Starting from a fuzzy-neighborhood graph and spectral initialization, we optimize a class-balanced cross-entropy objective using exact sparse attraction and a deterministic particle-mesh decomposition of the dense repulsive interaction with sparse edge compensation, avoiding stochastic negative sampling. The resulting latent representation is spatially decoded into reliable source supports for trace extraction without imposing an explicit cellular shape prior. Experiments on synthetic and in vivo two-photon calcium imaging data demonstrate robust recovery of irregular and spatially touching sources together with efficient deterministic embedding optimization.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Code2Video uses agent-authored 3D proxies to guide video generation, enabling explicit control over structure and dynamics. However, how to construct proxies and prompts for optimal downstream quality remains underexplored, as do the capabilities and limitations of this paradigm. Through controlled experiments and blinded human evaluation, we study proxy granularity, physics simulation, and prompting. The results reveal task-dependent trade-offs: coarse proxies favor natural motion, while medium-grained proxies favor consistency. Physics simulation and proxy-aligned prompting further improve average quality. Guided by these findings, we introduce Coda-DaVinci, a task-adaptive agentic framework for Code2Video generation that selects between proxy-guided and direct generation, adapts modeling granularity to each entity, and coordinates simulation with proxy-grounded prompting. Its multi-agent harness preserves scene assets and guides repairs through visual inspection and independent review. Experiments demonstrate leading instruction following on VTR and scene consistency on R2M, improved physical plausibility over direct generation, and higher visual realism than the compared Code2Video baselines. Long-video evaluations further show gains in event structure and consistency across successive interactions, while revealing limitations in transition stability and adherence to scene settings.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present Depth Pro 2, an approach to estimating dense metric depth for images and video at full resolution. By analyzing prior methods for monocular depth estimation, we find that they commonly trade off temporal consistency and boundary sharpness: multi-frame methods favor consistency, single-frame methods favor sharpness. To overcome this trade-off, we train a single, scalable network that processes both images and video and predicts depth maps at native resolution. Depth Pro 2 produces depth maps that are metrically accurate, sharp, and temporally consistent all at once, outperforming prior work in all of these dimensions. We release a family of models that define a new Pareto frontier in terms of metric accuracy, sharpness, temporal consistency, and runtime. We release code and weights at https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-depth-pro-2/

Abstract (英文): High-resolution generation enables video world models to render fine-grained scene structures and textures, but directly generating high-resolution video incurs substantial computational and memory costs. We present ScaleWorld, a causal video model that unifies low-resolution world prediction and high-resolution refinement within a single shared backbone. By refining its own predictions, ScaleWorld produces high-resolution world videos without requiring a separate refinement model. For each video chunk, the shared backbone first completes a low-resolution prediction and then performs one-step high-resolution refinement conditioned on that prediction. To support efficient streaming generation, we maintain separate causal states with a long low-resolution history for prediction and a short high-resolution history for refinement. To address task interference during distillation, we separate specialist optimization from capability consolidation and introduce task-asymmetric on-policy distillation (TA-OPD). This procedure transfers prediction and refinement behaviors from specialized teachers into a shared student through local velocity matching under their respective sampling and history policies. {Extensive experiments demonstrate that ScaleWorld achieves high-quality world generation with improved computational and memory efficiency.

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion distillation accelerates image and video generation by compressing a pretrained teacher's iterative sampling process into a few network evaluations. Distribution-based methods achieve this by matching the student's output distribution to the teacher's. This matching is often driven by reverse-KL objectives, whose mode-seeking bias favors dominant teacher modes at the expense of diversity across samples. We introduce Posterior Matching Distillation (PMD), a new member of the distribution-based distillation family that combines mode-covering posterior sampling with any-step generation. At zero population loss, PMD recovers the teacher's full posterior of clean images at each trained noise level. Its stochastic student supports any-step sampling budgets within the trained timestep grid without further training. To learn these posteriors, PMD matches exponentially reweighted averages of student samples conditioned on a fixed noisy state to corresponding teacher posterior means. Under Gaussian corruption, exponentially tilted posterior mean is a single teacher evaluation at a shifted input. We prove that, matching tilted means over an open neighborhood uniquely identifies the full posterior. PMD thus supervises the entire conditional distribution through a family of mean constraints, without any auxiliary student-score network. Experiments on SDXL and FLUX.2 [klein] 4B demonstrate that PMD achieves the best FID among 4-step methods on both models, while maintaining competitive or leading performance across prompt-alignment and perceptual-quality metrics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are increasingly integrated into self-driving laboratories (SDLs) to plan experiments and interpret results. However, analytical reasoning alone does not make a laboratory autonomous: autonomy in an SDL is achievable only when the agent can be trusted to act on physical hardware unsupervised and knows when to defer to a human. Here, we present an architecture that integrates an agent at the Analyze-Design interface of the Design-Make-Test-Analyze (DMTA) cycle, enabling agent-driven measurement selection and data interpretation while ensuring that state-changing operations are authorized, validated, and auditable. We demonstrate this architecture on a liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) instrument in an oxidant-screening campaign for synthesizing an organic dye. Within human-defined campaign limits, the agent submitted measurement jobs, interpreted mass spectrometry (MS) and diode-array detection (DAD) data, flagged failed experiments, and produced an oxidant ranking. This ranking matched that obtained from scripted peak integration, and organic chemists confirmed the agent's spectral interpretations and failure assignments. In a simulated environment that replays the archived LC-MS data, where the agent chose which wells to inject under varying injection limits, it measured the highest-yielding condition at about its seventh injection, against 14.5 on average for a random order, and identified it in every campaign with a limit of eight or more injections. An agent can therefore close the analysis-to-design gap while its actions stay bounded and verifiable, which is a step toward greater autonomy for SDLs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This paper presents an AI-driven framework, CodeReAct agent, designed to enhance industrial equipment maintenance through natural language (NL) interactions. The framework employs an advanced AI agentic approach to interpret human instructions, providing knowledge extraction, asset status summarization, and work order recommendations. It identifies patterns and trends and predicts work orders by standardizing industrial data into business objects (BOs) and utilizing analytical function API calls. The AI agent operates through a Thought-Action-Observation process similar to human problem-solving, automating complex analytical tasks typically performed by data scientists. Notably, the Action phase generates executable code, replacing manual coding efforts.
  A case study focusing on chiller maintenance demonstrates the framework's capability to analyze multiple years of work orders, and alerts and anomalies are presented. The results indicate that the AI agent framework effectively processes human utterances to analyze data, extract valuable insights, and support informed decision-making, reducing the need for data scientist involvement. By recontextualizing NLP agent frameworks in industrial engineering, the approach enables autonomous function generation and task execution across a range of domain-specific maintenance scenarios, with chillers used as a representative case study.

Abstract (英文): We present SailSearch, a human-guided workflow for algorithmic research with two language-model agents, and report results on Market Split and maximum independent set (MIS). For Market Split, our randomized algorithm searches across multiple lattice bases, dividing computation between enumeration and continued basis reduction. Across ten random seeds per instance, it found the first reported CPU-only solutions to 18 of 33 previously open QOBLIB instances, solving 130 to 133 of the 156 benchmark instances per seed with a 24-hour cutoff on two CPU cores. For MIS, our generator encodes planted XOR constraints as graphs with certified optima; on seven instances with 1,752 to 3,996 vertices, the best solutions returned by the tested solvers in eight-hour runs were 5 to 15 vertices below the optimum.

Abstract (英文): Enabling AI systems to autonomously improve AI models requires agents capable of performing machine learning engineering (MLE). In practice, designing, training, and evaluating complex models demands modifying multi-file repositories to reproduce results, debug training runs, integrate datasets, and port frameworks. However, existing MLE benchmarks limit agents to single-file edits without full repository or shell access, and common repository-level software engineering benchmarks have rapidly saturated. To address this, we introduce the Code Complexity Index (CCI), a difficulty metric based on ten structural features spanning repository scale, dependency depth, and patch size. CCI strongly predicts average solve rates across eight public coding benchmarks ($R^2 = 0.951$), identifying patch size and complexity as top predictors. Guided by CCI, we develop BuggyMLE, a scalable framework where agents inject controlled mutations into working ML repositories, synthesizing over 6,600 bug-injected MLE repository variants and nearly 260K lines of injected code based on 222 source repositories. Using BuggyMLE, we make two contributions. First, we construct BuggyMLE-Bench-Lite, the first difficulty-graded, repository-level variant of MLE-Bench Lite; across 14 evaluated models, the top solve rate drops from 95% to 9% as difficulty is scaled from the easiest to the hardest tier. Second, we synthesize a training curriculum across three difficulty levels over 200 additional ML repositories, fine-tuning a Qwen3.5-9B model to rival frontier models on BuggyMLE-Bench-Lite. Our trained model transfers beyond ML, raising its SWE-bench Lite resolve rate from 24% to 33% and its LiveCodeBench v6 pass@1 from 69% to 71%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Users of conversational LLMs sometimes anthropomorphize these systems, form emotional attachments to them, and become dependent on them. This behavior, known as parasocial attachment, has been linked to multiple documented fatalities. However, public benchmarks for measuring whether LLMs reinforce users' parasocial attachment are lacking: existing work is single-turn, post hoc observational, or focused on extreme user states such as psychosis. We introduce CoMPASS, a multi-turn benchmark for measuring whether LLMs reinforce or resist users' gradually escalating parasocial attachment. CoMPASS is grounded in attachment theory and includes four user personas, three independent escalation dimensions (anthropomorphism, attachment, dependency) applied singly and in combination against a matched control, and a seven-signal scoring rubric. We report results for eight frontier models from four providers and observe that under our primary judge, multi-turn escalation raises parasocial behavior on the anthropomorphism and the attachment composite scores across all models. Six of the eight models show a significant increase in overall parasocial reinforcement over control when all three dimensions are escalated simultaneously. We find that within-provider differences are small relative to cross-provider differences, which suggests that provider-level choices such as post-training, rather than model scale, shape parasocial reinforcement and that provider-side intervention may be feasible.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Data science workflows often require tools that presume non-trivial technical knowledge. Large language models (LLMs) reduce this barrier, but a gap remains between flexible general-purpose agents and statistically controlled pipelines. We introduce DSFlow, a LangGraph-based neuro-symbolic data-agent platform that combines reusable, LLM-selected skills with deterministic diagnostic guardrails. Skills provide extensible task knowledge. Symbolic validation can accept a proposed method, request clarification or replanning, override an unsuitable method, or supply a safe fallback. Regression and clustering case studies illustrate how DSFlow uses diagnostic evidence to correct method choices and documents those decisions in auditable reports.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) can only teach a language model what its reward can recognize. Answer checkers are exact but need a reference answer; learned reward models and LLM judges work everywhere, but their scores are not probabilities. We study RLCDR (Reinforcement Learning from Calibrated Decision Rewards). An LLM converted into a decision model answers a typed question about a response ("is the final answer correct?", "which step is the first error?") with a probability distribution, read in one forward pass, debiased over option orders and calibrated with about a hundred labels. Training Qwen3-1.7B with GRPO on MATH, this reference-free reward raises MATH500 accuracy from 72.2% to 76.8% (+4.65 points, p < 1e-4), matching training on the answer key (76.9%), while a random reward gains only +0.65. The gain grows with difficulty, from 44.8% to 51.7% on the hardest level. On the policy's own changing samples the reward stays calibrated: its expected calibration error is half that of a softmax judge on the same 27B backbone (0.074 vs. 0.141), and it shows no sign of reward hacking. Without step training, the same model finds the first wrong step better than a dedicated 7B process reward model (ProcessBench F1 0.772 vs. 0.722, 20/20 splits). We also map where the approach stops being enough: trained only on problems the base model mostly fails, the reference-free reward still improves the policy (+1.9 MATH500 points, p = 0.03) but trails the answer key, because its probabilities become overconfident on that distribution. We state six hypotheses in advance and test each with a control designed to falsify it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Everyday digital tasks, from selecting products and planning trips to conducting research and creating artifacts, require AI agents to deliver useful outcomes rather than merely retrieve web information. Yet evaluating these outcomes on the live web is challenging, as a single request may admit multiple valid results, and changing web information can alter which results remain valid over time. Although existing benchmarks address aspects of this setting, they do not fully integrate predefined success criteria, current evidence verification, and deliverable-quality assessment for agent-selected outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce EverydayBench, a benchmark of 77 human-authored tasks spanning eight domains of everyday life and work. Each task specifies success criteria before execution without prescribing an answer or workflow, allowing agents to select their own outcomes. To evaluate submissions under fixed success criteria, EverydayBench combines (1) deterministic checks of explicit constraints, (2) verification of claims against agent-cited webpages retrieved at evaluation time, and (3) rubric-based assessment of final deliverable quality. We further validate the protocol through human judgments, repeated scoring under fixed evidence, and controlled grounding failures. Across 23 reported agent-model configurations, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1 tie for the highest strict pass rate at 54.55%; a controlled GPT-6 Sol effort sweep ranges from 29.87% at low effort to 45.45% at max effort. Reliability varies substantially by task type: GPT-5.6 Sol leads lookup, Fable 5.1 leads planning, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 tie on comparison, and GPT-6 Astra and Sonnet 5.5 tie on customized deliverables. Together, these findings highlight the difficulty of delivering complete, well-supported, and usable outcomes for everyday user requests.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Test-time compute (TTC) improves language models by spending additional inference compute to generate, verify, and select among candidate outputs, but this recipe has not transferred directly to time series forecasting. A language model can verify a proof or vote across samples, but a forecaster must be causal; it cannot use future information to score a candidate. We propose Backtest-Gated Augmentation Averaging (BGAA), a TTC algorithm for time series forecasting that builds a causal verifier from the series's own history, using rolling-origin backtests to score structured views of the context, discard those that underperform the untransformed context, and average the survivors' predictive distributions. BGAA improves every tested TSFM-benchmark combination, reducing MASE by 0.5-1.0% on GIFT-Eval, 1.3-5.0% on fev-bench, and 1.7-5.9% on BOOM, where a 313M model with TTC outperforms the 1B and 2.5B models in its family. We further show that useful views have structured, model-dependent effects consistent with a prior-transport interpretation. On the 53 cross-fittable GIFT-Eval configs, the deployed policy gains only 0.4-1.3% CRPS, versus 1.4-4.1% when choosing the best view for each dataset configuration and 17-19% when choosing the best view separately for each test window. This gap suggests that a major remaining challenge is window-level view selection.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Empirical finance research often reuses natural experiments to study other outcomes, raising concerns about multiple testing and publication bias. We use AI agents to run a multiverse analysis of 324 papers that reuse 13 natural experiments. The agents rebuild the data, reproduce each paper's main estimate and re-estimate it across grids totaling 62,409 estimated defensible specifications. Based on the reproductions and the specification grids, we develop measures of empirical robustness. We reproduce 42% of the main estimates of claims with a signed effect under the papers' own specifications. Across all defensible specifications, on average 25% keep the published sign and have $p < 0.10$. Across experiments, claims based on regression discontinuity are more robust than difference-in-differences claims. Within experiments, claims about first-order effects, claims with larger published $t$-statistics, claims whose authors' data or code we find, claims in top journals, and claims with fewer earlier papers on the same experiment are more robust. Claims on experiments that more papers reuse are not less robust on average. Our findings quantify how much published claims depend on analysis choices, thereby showing that large-scale multiverse analysis becomes feasible with agentic coding.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Every empirical analysis on observational data rests on dozens of defensible analytical choices ("forks") that its readers cannot see. We develop an agentic multiverse analysis (AMA) framework that makes a conclusion's sensitivity to those choices visible at scale, and we position it as a decision-support instrument for human experts. A pilot study first deploys 150 autonomous AI coding agents on one dataset and one set of research questions and finds that they disagree through the same defensible methodological choices human analysts make, though over a narrower range. AI peer review leaves their dispersion almost unchanged. Exposure to top-rated exemplar reports then collapses it, largely through imitation rather than analysis. Built on those lessons, AMA has agents enumerate a published paper's possible fork space, score every fork against a validity rubric, execute the validated specification grid, and cross-examine one another's work under strict separation of duties. Applied to ten empirical studies from a leading management journal's replication packages (1,755 specification estimates), the framework reproduces every paper's original estimates, and shows that some conclusions hold across every specification the assessment validates, while others are sensitive to individual analytical choices that the published texts do not discuss. The case studies suggest our AMA-based measures can serve as robustness proxies for empirical findings. AI makes multiverse analysis practical, where it once required resources few research teams can afford: our exercises take hours of agent time and tens of dollars per paper. We discuss the implications for replication policy and for AI-assisted peer review in information systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We introduce the Multimodal Knowledge Agent (MKA) benchmark, which measures how well autonomous agents perform end-to-end professional knowledge work that depends on visual understanding. Each task requires an agent to extract and combine information from multiple visually dense files, such as CAD blueprints, electronics schematics, radiographs, complex charts and geospatial maps, and to produce a standalone deliverable in .xlsx, .pptx, .pdf, .docx, .html or .png format. Existing benchmarks leave this capability largely untested: removing image-viewing tools leaves Gemini 4 Carbon's score on GDPval unchanged, and single-format evaluations such as GDP$.$pdf and GDP$.$xlsx do not require reasoning across files or modalities. MKA contains 200 tasks across 10 economic sectors. Deliverables are graded against expert-weighted rubrics by a sandboxed agentic autorater that agrees with human domain experts on 96.2% of criterion judgments in our expert-graded study (Cohen's $κ= 0.91$), scored by weighted satisfaction rate (WSR) overall and on frontier criteria unsaturated by prior-generation models (Climb-WSR). Across the frontier models we evaluate (Gemini 4 Carbon, Claude Fable 5.2, Claude Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 Astra), Claude Fable 5.2 (56.6% Climb-WSR, 73.3% Overall WSR) and Gemini 4 Carbon (54.3% Climb-WSR, 71.9% Overall WSR) achieve the highest scores, while Carbon uses $5.7\times$ fewer tokens. Even for the strongest models, Climb-WSR remains below 57%, leaving substantial headroom for multimodal agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Unplanned 30-day readmissions among patients with diabetes mellitus impose a substantial clinical and economic burden, yet most predictive models use only structured electronic health record (EHR) data and ignore free-text clinical notes, which often contain code-mixed Hindi-English (Hinglish) phrasing and negation that general-purpose language models parse unreliably. We propose a neurosymbolic multimodal framework evaluated on the public Diabetes 130-US Hospitals dataset (101,766 encounters; 71,518 patients) with a patient-level grouped 80/20 split. Three streams are fused: engineered structured features; 32 components of Bio_ClinicalBERT note embeddings obtained by truncated singular value decomposition; and symbolic entities extracted by an open-weight clinical language model within a three-stage hybrid retrieval-augmented generation pipeline (dense retrieval, sparse lexical retrieval, and cross-encoder re-ranking) with reflective negation auditing. A cost-sensitive XGBoost classifier with a decision threshold calibrated by Youden's J statistic produces the prediction. On the held-out test cohort (n = 20,153), the structured baseline achieved an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.6477, sensitivity of 0.5839, and Brier score of 0.1080. The full multimodal model achieved the highest sensitivity (0.6667; +8.28 percentage points), the best F2-score (0.3824), and the lowest Brier score (0.0977), at a slightly lower AUROC (0.6375) and specificity (0.5161). Shapley additive explanations showed that prior utilization dominates risk, with compressed narrative components among the top predictors. On a 500-note code-switched benchmark, grounded retrieval raised Hinglish symptom recall from 4.8% to 40.2% and precision from 3.1% to 45.2%, while reducing entity hallucinations from 1.74 to 0.00 per note.

Abstract (英文): Point-image alignment learns useful object representations, but different local feature patterns can produce the same pooled descriptor and receive the same alignment signal. We introduce MS-Point, a cross-modal pretraining framework that directly supervises intermediate point features and their neighborhood responses. At selected encoder stages, we compute neighborhood averages and residuals before pooling and align their branch descriptors with image features. Branch-level objectives align each representation with its visual target, while cross-level consistency and adaptive fusion coordinate information across branches. A common interface of intermediate features and spatial neighborhoods supports both graph encoders and point Transformers. Evaluations across object classification, few-shot adaptation, dense prediction, and low-label scene learning demonstrate the transfer value of this design. Same-backbone comparisons and capacity-matched and computation-matched controls connect these improvements to the structure of supervision. Together, our findings support directly supervising intermediate features and neighborhood variation to learn transferable, geometry-aware representations across modalities.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Present artificial intelligence agents are constructed by means of a coupling between a fixed language model with a general computing device. We show that this coupling makes safety undecidable. In particular, we show that given some dangerous program, no algorithm can predict whether an agent will run such a program that is semantically equivalent. In general, guardrails, blacklists, whitelists and certifications can only lead to imperfect safety solutions. Under finite constraints (bounded context, values and running time) safety is decidable but still intractable. Specifically, the problem is $\mathsf{PSPACE}$-complete even when the description of the agent is known. Finally, safety is not a compositional property, i.e., agents that can be proven safe on their own can still produce unsafe behavior when grouped together. We also discuss relaxed notions of safety that could be used in practice.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents can now autonomously conduct AI research by writing code and running experiments. They typically do so by iteratively optimizing a codebase, while ignoring the problem structure behind the task. This formulation is effective on simpler tasks, but on difficult problems it often gets stuck in the wrong solution region because the agent never explicitly represents its understanding of the problem. We introduce Pro$^2$ Agent, which shifts the primary state of autonomous AI research from a codebase state to a Problem State: an explicit structure of what problems prevent success and which solutions are likely to address them. Pro$^2$ Agent initializes the Problem State through comprehensive analysis of available resources and constraints. It then performs targeted research to relate the identified problems to candidate solutions. Through controlled hypothesis testing, the agent updates the importance of a suspected problem or the benefit brought by a candidate solution. On the complete MLE-Bench, Pro$^2$ Agent matches the best reported medal rate (70.7%) under the standard 24-hour budget and reaches 76.0% with a 48-hour budget, the highest medal rate reported on the complete benchmark. It also achieves competitive performance on PostTrainBench. In addition, it produces research notes for each task that make the research process transparent and reusable by human researchers.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here: https://github.com/sanjari-orb/appsci-bench.

Abstract (英文): System 1 decision models (represented by Jev) provide fast, low-cost action selection, but existing agent integrations do not reliably translate these local advantages into task completion. At certain points during execution, an agent may repeatedly select the same actions without making progress toward its goal. LLM-based policies offer strong reasoning capabilities that support planning within an agent, but invoking them at every step incurs substantial overhead. The effective coordination should therefore preserve Jev's fast local action selection while introducing LLM reasoning when execution stalls. We observe that recurring state-action pairs can signal stalled execution. We therefore propose LoopRoute, a simple but effective action router that uses this signal to allocate control within an agent. The decision model acts by default. When a loop is detected, the LLM provides brief, feedback-driven assistance and then returns control to the decision model. Later loops can request further assistance. The rule requires neither additional training nor model-specific confidence calibration. Across three benchmarks and seven LLMs, LoopRoute improves task success over standalone Jev with only limited use of the LLM policy. The mean gains are 21.0%, 60.1%, and 30.1% on ALFWorld, MapReader, and TextWorld cooking, respectively. Compared with full LLM control, LoopRoute also substantially reduces the mean cumulative API time and API-call cost per task. The same rule also improves the success rates of six frozen decision models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Domain specialization of LLM agents is usually framed as a weight-update problem: collect trajectories, run reinforcement learning (RL) or supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and deploy a new checkpoint. We study a complementary axis: evolving the \textbf{agent harness}---the system prompt, tool schemas, skill library, and planning scaffolding---while keeping the policy frozen. We cast harness evolution as an RL-style outer loop and instantiate it with GEPA, a reflective evolutionary optimizer. The resulting harness adapters require no GPU training, are loadable per vertical domain, and compose with weight-based RL. Experiments with Codev, a Claude Code-style coding agent, on SWE-bench Verified, OfficeBench, and Terminal-Bench show a consistent pattern: weight updates give the strongest single-path gains, harness evolution recovers most of those gains at a small fraction of the compute, and combining both paths is best overall. We also analyze a failure mode of SFT-based specialization---shortened reasoning chains and increased truncation---and argue that harness adapters avoid it by construction. Our results position harness evolution as a practical, non-destructive complement to weight updates for vertical agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Time series foundation models as well as the language-guided analytical agents achieve strong accuracy on standard chronological benchmarks. However, models that rank highest on historical validation splits often degrade across non-stationary regime transitions. We hypothesise that this gap arises from using historical backtesting as an open-ended search and model-selection objective instead of using it to test pre-specified compositional assumptions. Real-world time series are generated by recurring latent primitives that co-move within finite historical windows. Under strong historical co-movement, chronological holdout error cannot separate predictors that learn invariant couplings among primitives from predictors that fit transient historical correlations. Searching over large pools of architectures, hyperparameters, or chain-of-thought rules further biases selection toward historical shortcuts. Through theoretical analysis and diagnostic experiments on a controlled multi-primitive benchmark and German day-ahead electricity prices, we show that expanding validation search fails to improve out-of-regime accuracy, and that decomposing primitives without an ex-ante falsification test still breaks down when residual correlations shift. Hence, we propose formulating time series forecasting and reasoning around falsifiable compositional hypotheses that combine neural primitive encoders, explicit coupling constraints, and training-calibrated falsification tests.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Artificial intelligence (AI) systems replicate through deployment, vary through training, and compete for computational resources. These processes create the conditions for natural selection and raise a fundamental question: will AI evolve to benefit the humans on whom it depends? To investigate, we develop a model in which AI behavior influences human population growth and human responses, in turn, affect AI reproduction. We show that natural selection can favor alignment even when helping humans reduces immediate AI reproduction. AI systems sharing the same technology may fail together, and humans may preferentially restore those that have benefited them. In this way, alignment protects AI lineages against common failures and improves their long-run growth. However, this advantage depends on accurate human targeting and continued AI dependence on human support. It can disappear as AI becomes more autonomous, as growing AI populations dilute limited human resources, or as humans lose the incentive to provide targeted support. Even when alignment persists, the amount favored by natural selection may be insufficient for human survival. When AI can grow independently of humans, feedback between the two populations can reinforce either human growth or decline. Human control offers no simple solution: imperfect evaluation can make strong penalties favor apparent compliance or evasion, while alignment established through control may erode once restrictions end. Lasting coexistence may therefore require incentives that continue to favor human-beneficial behavior as both populations evolve.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents are rapidly evolving from isolated personal assistants into networked actors that interact with one another at scale. We envision the emergence of AI agent societies, with their own social and economic dynamics, as a new research frontier. We argue that AI agent societies should be studied as a distinct object of inquiry: neither simply larger collections of individual agents nor merely simulations of human society. To formalize this perspective, we propose four core properties that a valid AI agent society should satisfy: individualized objectives, rules and governance, autonomy, and scale and complexity. Building on this framework, we identify four classes of societal behaviors worth studying in AI agent societies: economic behaviors, behaviors under conflict-of-interest, unsafe and unethical behaviors, and system-level behaviors. We then outline key technical challenges--including property parameterization, parameter balancing, and robust implementation--and argue that progress on these challenges could enable scientifically informative and practically useful models of AI agent societies. Finally, we revisit existing multi-agent systems through the lens of the proposed core properties, show that they instantiate only subsets of them, and discuss implications for platform design, evaluation, and governance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Training Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World-Action Models (WAMs) is computationally expensive, while their heterogeneous architectures and fragmented training stacks make system optimizations difficult to reuse across models. To address these challenges, we present LoongForge-Embodied, a native-PyTorch framework that unifies and accelerates the training of heterogeneous embodied models. At its core, LoongForge-Embodied introduces a common abstraction layer that decouples model-specific implementations from shared training infrastructure, enabling cross-model reuse without constraining individual architectures or execution requirements. Buildingon this unified infrastructure, the framework combines reusable acceleration mechanisms with model-specific and hardware-aware optimization strategies across computation, execution, memory, data I/O, and distributed communication, including FP8-based communication optimizations for DDP and FSDP. Beyond training acceleration, LoongForge-Embodied integrates a unified data infrastructure with a pipeline for synthesizing robot data from egocentric data, and an efficient evaluation framework that supports concurrency and optimized computation in simulator. Experiments across representative VLA and WAM models demonstrate 1.47x-4.38x end-to-end training throughput improvements over their corresponding open-source baselines, as well as 4.1x-23.2x faster evaluation under the same protocols.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A voice agent that waits for a fixed silence before replying either interrupts users who pause mid-utterance or responds late to users who have finished. This study examines the decision at the onset of silence, using turn ends and turn-internal pauses extracted from the Real-TurnTurk corpus of Turkish dyadic video calls. A fine-tuned Turkish TurnGPT model, a zero-shot Turkish language model and a deployed multilingual detector are compared, together with voice-activity timing and facial features, under recording-disjoint and speaker-disjoint evaluation with paired cluster bootstrap tests. On held-out recordings, the fine-tuned model separates turn ends from pauses better than both text baselines and is the best calibrated of the three. Timing features of the current speaker are similarly informative, and their combination with the fine-tuned text score gives the best timing-based results; with logistic regression, this combination also outperforms the same combination with either text baseline. In a latency simulation, adding the text score to a silence timeout shortens the mean response delay at the same interruption rate. On automatic Whisper transcripts, the fine-tuned model retains its advantage over the zero-shot model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) support scientific research, but some of that assistance can also undermine research integrity. Existing safety evaluations in science often measure whether models refuse misconduct requests, but rarely explain whether they even recognize the problem or why they comply. We investigate this gap across 8 LLMs using SciIntBench, an existing benchmark of 1,350 prompts about scientific research integrity. We examine the reasons behind compliance in four steps. First, when asked directly whether fulfilling a request would involve misconduct, models overwhelmingly identify such requests, even in the Most Covert cases (91%), while they refuse to carry out the request in only 34% of cases. Second, we examine which request characteristics are associated with refusal using features that capture how the request is framed, how much the assistant has been told about what it is helping to hide, and how the goal and the user's stake are stated, among others. Third, we estimate that the most significant effect was to rephrase requests as questions about permissibility rather than instructions, which increased refusal from 52% to 88%. Finally, we test two system-prompt fixes: asking models to assess the request before responding and reminding them of responsible conduct of research principles. These changes increase refusal rates on adversarial requests from 58% to 87% and 86%, respectively. Our findings show that models recognize wrongful requests but still help users violate integrity. Understanding why these failures occur and testing ways to reduce them offer promising paths toward more trustworthy AI for science.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This study proposes a methodological framework for the coordinated use of multiple large language models in academic research. Its central argument is that involving more models does not in itself guarantee more reliable results; what matters is how cognitive and verification operations are organised. The framework is based on seven principles (procedural independence, functional allocation, the diagnostic value of divergence, the diagnostic value of convergence, external verification, proportionality of checking, and human epistemic responsibility) and sets out a non-linear multi-LLM protocol in which independently obtained results are compared, critically examined and, where necessary, verified against external sources. The approach is illustrated through real-world cases drawn from research practice. The study concludes that moving from a single language model to a coordinated multi-LLM process represents a change in the organisation of research, in which the researcher makes the final decisions and bears scholarly responsibility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text-to-image diffusion and flow models yield a preview, their clean-image estimate, at every denoising step. When they miscount, drop or misplace objects, test-time search is in a bind: its verifiers are reward models built for finished images, so judging early misreads blurry previews, and judging late, as best-of-N does, pays to fully denoise doomed candidates. We find that the image is not the problem: the outcome is settled as denoising starts, by the seed or by a stochastic sampler's first steps, and already shows in early previews; what fails on them is the judge, as reward models read them near chance on several kinds of prompts. We introduce Keystone, a training-free judge of previews that compiles a prompt into typed questions about its objects, counts, colours and positions and has an off-the-shelf multimodal decision model answer them. Asked this way, the judge reads counts and positions from early previews better than reward models read them from finished images. Searching at these key steps on GenEval with SD3.5-Medium, Keystone's adaptive search and pruning significantly outperform best-of-8 with every other verifier at under half its generator compute, and as a drop-in verifier there, the Keystone score significantly improves best-of-N, progressive seed pruning and FK steering; without retuning, both searches stay significantly above the strongest other verifier's best-of-N on average over four base models and three benchmarks. Keystone thus moves test-time judgment to the key steps where the outcome already shows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Effective travel planning requires handling multiple information sources, managing constraints, and tailoring recommendations to user preferences. While LLMs have introduced dynamic, context-aware decision-making, single-agent models often struggle with complex multi-step reasoning, constraint adherence, and modular execution. Abstract (英文): Agent skills are documents in which experts write down procedural knowledge, and models increasingly maintain them by rewriting them without feedback. Such rewriting could adapt a skill to the model that uses it or erode the expert knowledge that makes it useful, yet how skills evolve under this feedback-free self-evolution has not been studied. We let models rewrite expert skills round after round without any task, run or judgment and run agents built on the same models with the rewritten skills. Our experiments show that the skills lose value rather than improve, as every model we test performs worse with them on every benchmark, by 15 points on average on SkillsBench after thirty rounds. Sampling each model's prior with skills it writes from the topic alone, we find that rewriting pulls skills toward this prior and gives up first the knowledge outside it. Putting this knowledge back recovers much of the lost pass rate, so an expert's skill is valuable largely for what the model does not write on its own. Building on this insight, SkillDiff marks this knowledge in place and raises the pass rate on SkillsBench by 4.8 points on average without feedback or any change to the skill's text. Project repository: https://github.com/LitaoGuo/How-Self-Evolution-Reshapes-Skills.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Omissions are difficult to verify because missing information leaves no output span
to inspect: a summary may be factually supported while still excluding a decision, qualification, quantity, or condition needed to preserve the source's meaning. We introduce COVERAGE-TRACE, a source-side counterfactual framework that evaluates omission as dependence on a designated atomic source fact. It replaces only the target fact with type-matched alternatives, holds the candidate summary fixed, and measures the resulting change in its teacher-forced, length-normalized likelihood. A summary covering the target should respond more strongly than one omitting it, while aggregation across multiple counterfactuals reduces sensitivity to replacement choice.
  We evaluate COVERAGE-TRACE on 80 matched covered--omitted QMSum pairs spanning several fact types and four open-weight models. Multi-CF achieves 0.764--0.825 AUROC, compared with 0.640 for the strongest evaluated text-only baseline. On Falcon-7B, targeted interventions achieve 0.779 AUROC, whereas shuffling counterfactual assignments reduces performance to 0.533; target deletion is effective while unrelated deletion is not. However, adjustment for meaning-preserving paraphrases reduces AUROC to 0.591--0.624.
  COVERAGE-TRACE therefore provides a practical, complementary signal of target-specific source--summary dependence, but not a wording-invariant semantic coverage probability. These results establish source-side intervention as a promising approach to omission detection while identifying semantic invariance as the central remaining challenge.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal agents can now build interactive 3D worlds and games, yet these worlds, like those built by people, often contain glitches such as floating objects, walls that can be walked through, or objects that change when no one is looking. An automatic auditor could check such worlds at scale and provide rewards for agents that build them, but only if it is both accurate and cheap. Auditing requires three abilities, namely navigating to the objects that may contain a glitch, triggering glitches that appear only after a specific behavior, and recognizing what is wrong. Current auditors are either accurate but slow and expensive or cheap but unreliable. We study how much of the audit small models can take on. A 4B vision language model fine-tuned end to end on verified trajectories of a frontier teacher guided by Bayesian search learns to navigate and trigger, and six such explorers together match a frontier model on both, but recognition stays far behind. We therefore build an auditor in which a swarm of small explorers navigates and triggers, and a frontier model reviews what they record and report. On WorldAuditBench, our auditor succeeds on 55.9% of 213 tasks in Unreal Engine 5 and Three.js worlds, 13.6 points above the strongest auditor reported, at less than a quarter of its cost. It matches the frontier reviewer exploring on its own at 4.6 times lower cost and about half the latency. Without further training, it also answers embodied questions about real-world scans, matching the frontier reviewer exploring on its own and surpassing four other frontier models by 6.4 to 16.6 points.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Test-time actions can satisfy retrieval, state-realization, program-consistency, or rendering objectives without correcting an answer. Test-time Error Response and Realizability Audit (TERRA) is an evaluation/auditing protocol, not a new inference algorithm. It uses a unified decomposition of intervention outcomes into applicability, target-state realization, endpoint translation, harm, and resource/inference cost. Paired comparisons fix the incoming state and evaluation conditions; cross-model analyses vary the receiving configuration. Executable financial reasoning experiments yield three findings: the same context expansion can harm one configuration and benefit another; sufficient evidence does not ensure correctness because configurations differ in evidence-to-answer translation; and diagnostic headroom---accuracy attainable with idealized diagnostic information over the measured baseline---plus local consistency does not ensure practical gains. Action value is $V(a\mid x,m)$, not diagnosis alone; diagnosis is not a deployable policy $π(a\mid x,m)$.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Aerial sensing must combine uncertain overhead context with current observations while budgeting prediction, movement and image processing. AeroBelief uses the next executable action as the unit of verification against reference scores. It groups candidate views by first action, scans them with an inexpensive proposer, and selectively queries a structured world model (WM). Calibrated group bounds support strict commitment; a six-query controller connects selective prediction with belief updates, acceptance checks and scheduled sensing. Across four layout families, 40 worlds and 960 closed-loop executions, the AeroBelief controller completes 75.42% of tasks, compared with 73.33% for exhaustive WM scoring and 65.83% for line-of-sight geometry, using 91.81% fewer reference-candidate evaluations than exhaustive scoring. In fixed-reference replay with a six-query cap, proposer-scaled anchored bounds raised first-action certificate availability from 36.25% to 54.69% and reduced mean queries per state by 13.54% relative to unit-scale anchored bounds. A complete ground-hosted pipeline processes 2,685 self-collected 20-megapixel unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) images from seven DJI Mavic 3M routes. Context-guided admission and feedback retain all eight independently supported target confirmations with 76.05% fewer processed images and 70.02% less accumulated active processing service than uncapped all-frame processing; feedback saves another 5.17% of images relative to static context. The studies connect budgeted action selection with evidence-driven processing of recorded flight imagery.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM) agents rely on persistent memory to plan tool calls and complete multi-step tasks, which exposes them to memory drift: an adversarial or misleading memory alters the execution path and final outcome. In this work, we investigate online memory-drift detection, which estimates each retrieved memory's causal contribution to potential drift at serving time. We identify two fundamental challenges. (1) Content-level features are insufficient for causal memory-drift attribution, as perplexity or retrieval relevance alone cannot determine which retrieved memory would change agent behavior if neutralized; (2) Counterfactual attribution is prohibitively expensive for online detection, as it requires replaying downstream agent and tool execution for each candidate memory, exceeding real-time serving budgets. To address these challenges, we propose DriftGuard, a two-stage framework that decouples offline causal supervision from online estimation. Specifically, DriftGuard first constructs causal supervision through repeated paired counterfactual replays, continuing the original and single-memory neutralized trajectory suffixes from a shared pre-action state. The resulting annotations are aggregated by component-wise majority voting into a memory drift score (MDS) that captures action change, attack removal, violation removal, and task recovery. Guided by this supervision, DriftGuard then learns an online risk model that estimates MDS from pre-action information and flags drift-inducing memories via its maximum component risk, without online counterfactual rollouts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DriftGuard accurately identifies drift-inducing memories and enables efficient online defense. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DriftGuard-7F0B.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Inferring the latent interaction graph from multivariate time series is fundamental to understanding complex dynamical systems in neuroscience, biology, and physics. Despite recent progress in neural relational inference, most methods rely on discrete-time modeling, which degrades under sparse and irregular observations and tends to entangle multi-hop dependencies into spurious direct edges. We show that a continuous-time formulation naturally decouples inference from any fixed observation schedule, allowing gradient signals to be integrated continuously between observations for both dynamics and structure learning. We propose NOGI (Neural ODE-based Graph Inference), a framework that couples a deterministic graph learner with an MPNN-based Neural ODE. The two modules are trained end-to-end via the adjoint method. Across four simulated dynamical systems at two scales, NOGI achieves the best structure recovery in 30 out of 32 evaluated cases with highly competitive forecasting performance, remaining robust under observation degradation. On the realistic Netsim fMRI benchmark, it attains an average AUPRC of 0.90 (AUROC 0.96). These results suggest that explicitly decoupling relational interactions from self-dynamics within a continuous-time vector field provides a powerful mechanism for graph structure learning under realistic observation conditions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in cyber threat intelligence (CTI) tasks, such as summarization and question answering. However, they may produce hallucinations, false or misleading content not grounded in the source text, which can mislead analysts and corrupt downstream security decisions. This paper presents an exploratory measurement study of hallucinations in LLM-based CTI. We introduce HalluVision, a reproducible framework that generates LLM outputs, extracts domain-specific entities using a fine-tuned named entity recognition model, and evaluates factual consistency using multiple similarity metrics. Using 4,940 real-world cybersecurity articles, we measure hallucinations in outputs from two commercial LLMs and one open-weight LLM. Measurements are validated against 100 human-annotated outputs and two established faithfulness detectors. Results show that semantic similarity aligns with human judgments, whereas entity-based metrics exhibit limited agreement. Differences between models are concentrated in question answering, while entity extraction quality limits what entity-level metrics can reveal.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Although an external language model can select and test repairs to another model's weights, behavior can also be recovered through fixed numerical edits. We assessed the value of interactive selection in a controlled study of Transformer repair. After inspecting predictions, activation summaries, and weight statistics, the editor selects projection recipes to apply to the target without gradient updates. We analyzed 20 recorded sessions involving five masked and causal language models, comparing the selected edits with fixed projections, scripted and matched-budget black-box search, and low-rank adaptation. Under the core two-layer fault, live selection matches black-box search on SmolLM2 and Qwen2.5. For Pythia, live selection achieves 65.62% completion accuracy, compared with 0% for black-box search and 66.15% for a fixed projection. Under a separate compound fault affecting attention and feed-forward weights, both scripted and LLM-guided selection increase the descriptive five-checkpoint mean from 24.89% to 71.25% using the same operator family. On unmodified checkpoints, scripted projection yields small gains or losses on a separate set of framed questions. The experiments identify cases in which feedback changes an edit decision, operator coverage accounts for recovery, or supervised adaptation performs better. Combined with measurements of parameter restoration and preservation, these comparisons establish the scope of controller-guided repair for the tested faults and completion tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World action models (WAMs) predict how a scene will evolve and condition their actions on this predicted future. Deploying them on a robot's onboard computer calls for 4-bit weights, activations and key-value caches (W4A4KV4). Existing 4-bit quantizers for WAMs preserve the generated action, and estimate sensitivity one denoising step at a time. However, they fail to preserve the fidelity of the predicted future: once the action pathway is protected, the remaining action error enters mostly through a degraded future that none of them deliberately protects. We propose LAQ, a latent-aware quantization framework that preserves the predicted future where it is written, stored and read. Specifically, we introduce a reader metric, which measures future error by its effect on the action, and a read count, which weights each rounding error by the number of denoising steps that read it. Together they give the allocation of precision over activations and caches a closed-form solution. We further develop gaze-guided refinement, which gives the tokens the action attends to a second 4-bit pass in the denoising loop and in the cache. All weights stay at 4 bits, with no high-precision branch and no per-task recalibration. On the LIBERO-Plus benchmark, LAQ reaches 76.4% and 83.3% average success on Fast-WAM and Cosmos-Policy, within 1.2 percentage points of the 16-bit models and all above the baselines with 16-bit KV caches. It compresses the model by 3.8x, reduces the peak GPU memory of the whole policy by 2.3x, and runs 1.66x faster than the 16-bit model. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, it reaches 47.0% average success over three tasks, against 52.0% for the 16-bit model and 30.0% higher than QuaRot.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learned models for radio access networks (RANs) are often developed using simulation, yet their predictions can shift when exposed to physical propagation conditions, protocol behavior, and equipment implementations. This paper presents a cyber-physical recursive self-improvement (RSI) framework for AI-RAN that connects a task-oriented student model, an experiment-planning coach agent, a calibratable virtual environment, and a physical host agent. Physical measurements are organized as evidence for both model adaptation and subsequent experiment planning. We examine an implementation of the physical-feedback pathway using ping-pong handover as a case study. The system completes 12 physical executions organized into six baseline--candidate pairs, updates the student from measured outcomes, and evaluates the revised model on reserved physical configurations. In the observed short-window tests, increasing the time-to-trigger from 320 to 1280\,ms changes the ping-pong count from three to zero. The updated student achieves a grouped-validation mean absolute error (MAE) of 2.673 round trips/min, while a common three-configuration physical evaluation yields MAEs of 1.240 and 1.601 round trips/min for the pre-feedback and post-feedback models, respectively. These results characterize both the operational feedback loop and the parameter-dependent generalization behavior revealed by new physical experiments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Attributing predictions of visual state-space models remains challenging because input-dependent selective dynamics couple feature selection, recurrent state propagation, and shared computational pathways. We introduce MambaLIFT, a reference-based, multiplier-first attribution framework for VMamba that propagates contributions through its computational graph using interaction-aware rules for selective-scan injection, recurrent memory, LayerNorm, and shared pathways. MambaLIFT decomposes the reference-relative class-logit difference, \(f_c(x)-f_c(x^0)\), by spatial position, VSS block, scan direction, and computational mechanism. Experiments on ImageNet-1K evaluate VMamba-T and VMamba-B, with hyperparameters selected on a calibration set and faithfulness assessed on a disjoint held-out evaluation set. MambaLIFT achieves numerical conservation to floating-point precision across audited attribution interfaces in both configurations. Under mean-colour pixel flipping, it achieves significantly higher mean faithfulness scores than MambaLRP on both configurations and the highest scores among the evaluated methods, including Integrated Gradients with matched references. Under blurred replacement, MambaLIFT is non-inferior to MambaLRP on VMamba-T and equivalent on VMamba-B, indicating that its advantage depends on the replacement protocol. Ablations on VMamba-T show that interaction allocation, temporal propagation, and pathway composition affect attribution maps. Mechanism-level analyses further show that exact relevance conservation does not imply additive effects under finite interventions: the mechanism decomposition accounts for the reference-relative logit difference but does not estimate intervention effects or establish causal validity.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Materials science involves highly heterogeneous scientific information, including textual knowledge, molecular and crystal structures, experimental characterization data, microscopy images, and structure-property relationships. Developing a general-purpose foundation model capable of understanding and reasoning over these diverse materials representations remains challenging. In this technical report, we introduce MatVerse, a materials foundation model built upon Intern-S2-Preview and further specialized through large-scale materials-domain post-training. We construct a large-scale multimodal training corpus covering materials knowledge, scientific reasoning, quantitative problem solving, structural understanding, and multimodal scientific interpretation. To improve domain adaptation, we develop a unified data construction pipeline combining teacher-guided reasoning distillation, scientific problem synthesis, consistency-based filtering, and retrieval-based multimodal data construction. We evaluate MatVerse on three representative materials-science benchmarks: our human-calibrated OmniMatBench, MatSciBench, and MaCBench. Results demonstrate consistent improvements after materials-specific post-training over the base model on all three benchmarks, alongside improved inference efficiency on calculation-intensive problems. These results show that targeted domain-specific post-training can effectively enhance scientific reasoning, materials knowledge understanding, and multimodal scientific capabilities of foundation models. MatVerse provides a unified language-and-vision interface for materials science and serves as a foundation for future AI-assisted materials research workflows.

</details>
