Abstract (英文): How should we make decisions under XORness? XORness is a class of uncertainty arising from indeterminacy, exclusivity, and hesitancy. We develop a multiple criteria decision making framework that preserves XORness throughout the decision process. We define XOR numbers and prove their fundamental arithmetic and algebraic properties. Building on these results, we extend the deterministic decision matrix to an XOR decision matrix and introduce a compatible normalization procedure. Further, we propose an additive XOR aggregation operator, which preserves indeterminacy, exclusivity, and hesitancy inherent in evaluations. In addition, we introduce an axiomatic XORness measure that quantifies the degree of uncertainty associated with global performance scores and develop a performance--XORness index to rank alternatives. We prove that this index induces a total preorder over the set of alternatives and identify the conditions for preserving dominance. The applicability of the developed framework is demonstrated through the risk assessment of cyberthreat scenarios in energy systems, and recommendations are derived using the XOR--weighted sum method.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reinforcement learning (RL) with electronic design automation (EDA) feedback is advancing large language models for hardware design and verification. Extending these gains to hardware-engineering agents requires environments that span tasks, preserve state across tool calls, and support large hierarchies alongside parallel rollouts. We present SiliconGym, the first versatile, cross-task and cross-toolchain RL environment for hardware design and verification. By decoupling versioned workspaces, budgeted execution, and acceptance-gated scoring, SiliconGym scales from module-level RTL, testbench, and PPA tasks to multi-round repository repair and full-hierarchy SoC flows. Streaming dispatch overlaps tool checks with generation, finishing a 256-answer RL batch 21.9% faster than native ChipSeek. Using interchangeable open-source and commercial backends, we show that open-source-trained functional correctness transfers cleanly to commercial simulation, while paired netlist evaluation reveals when open-source PPA gains survive commercial synthesis.

Abstract (英文): Large language models are increasingly deployed as LLM-as-a-Service (LLMaaS), where user queries are sent to remote LLMs for inference, raising severe privacy concerns about the exposure of sensitive user inputs. Differentially private inference mitigates these risks by injecting noise into input embeddings. However, existing methods struggle to balance privacy and utility, as stronger privacy protection often leads to substantial accuracy degradation in LLM inference.
  In this work, we propose DP-SPD, a differentially private LLM inference framework that protects user queries by hiding the real query among client-generated dummy queries. All queries are randomly shuffled before being sent to the server, preventing direct identification of the user's true intent. To improve inference efficiency, we exploit the shared-prefix structure among queries and reuse transformer key--value (KV) caches during decoding. We analyze the privacy guarantee of DP-SPD and evaluate its utility on classification and generation tasks, as well as its privacy protection under QIA, EIA, and GPT-based attacks. Experimental results show that our method achieves strong inference-time privacy protection and near-plaintext utility while improving inference efficiency through shared-prefix KV-cache reuse.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Efficient chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs) requires reducing both intermediate reasoning steps and their execution cost. Latent CoT methods compress textual rationales into continuous thoughts, yet their full-precision (FP) models retain substantial storage and computation costs. Lowering numerical precision could reduce these remaining costs, yet direct quantization severely degrades reasoning accuracy, particularly with 4-bit weights and activations. Operator and perturbation analyses reveal key sensitivity and direction-dependent predictive effects of continuous-thought errors, motivating preservation of information retrieval and subsequent reasoning beyond reconstruction accuracy. We therefore introduce QL-VLM, a dynamics-aware Quantization framework for efficient Latent reasoning in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). First, Dynamics-Guided Quantization (DGQ) uses nonuniform codebooks that preserve rotary-key structure, mitigating quantization-induced distortion in vision-language information retrieval. Second, Dynamics-Preserving Distillation (DPD) aligns continuous thoughts, latent-query readouts, and predictions with a full-precision teacher, maintaining predictive consistency through subsequent low-precision reasoning. On ScienceQA-IMG with Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, 4-bit QL-VLM achieves 90.43% accuracy and improves baseline by 40.33%, with 16.64x fewer bit-operations and 6.40x lower measured latency than explicit CoT.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Early interactions can reveal physical properties that later observations no longer show. These properties can still gov- ern the effects of future actions. Inspired by the distinction between retention and selective readout in working mem- ory and cognitive science, we introduce LATER, an action- conditioned world model built on CF-JEPA. LATER stores completed real transitions in partitioned memories that remain fixed during candidate rollouts. A response bridge queries these memories using the current state and candidate action, while multi-cut auxiliary supervision trains the memory writ- ers. Abstract (英文): As language-model agents are applied to increasingly extended tasks, their working context continuously accumulates interaction history, tool observations, intermediate decisions, and task progress. Context compaction offers a practical way to reduce this growing history while allowing execution to continue, yet the compaction ability of modern LLMs remains insufficiently understood. We introduce CompAct-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating working-context compaction through its downstream effect on agent task completion. Starting from successful agent trajectories, we insert compaction boundaries at different stages of execution, compress the preceding working context with the model under evaluation, and let the same executor continue the original task. To enable fair comparison across compactors, we evaluate them under controlled retention ratios using a lightweight iterative ratio-adjustment procedure. CompAct-Bench covers information-seeking, software-engineering, and workspace-oriented tasks through BrowseComp, SWE-bench, and an internal CompanyBench evaluation. Our experiments show that working-context compaction remains highly lossy even for frontier models: Claude- and GPT-family models perform strongest overall, yet substantial degradation in downstream task accuracy persists under aggressive compaction. Further analysis reveals that failures are often caused not only by omitted information, but also by distorted task state, such as turning unresolved issues into apparent conclusions or forgetting previously established results, which can lead to incorrect decisions or redundant execution. These findings highlight working-context compaction as a distinct and still challenging ability for LLMs operating in extended agent tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on visual feedback throughout manipulation, making them vulnerable to persistent texture attacks. However, optimized patterns can stand out from their surroundings, making an attack easier to notice and its carrier more likely to be inspected or removed. To address this limitation, our key insight is to search the structured image distribution of a diffusion model for natural-looking textures while using closed-loop feedback to identify task-disruptive candidates. Based on this idea, we propose DMT-Attack, a gradient-free gray-box attack that generates textures for ordinary scene objects such as tablecloths. A frozen text-conditioned diffusion model defines the search space, and an eight-expert latent proposal network is initialized from evaluated teacher latents and refined using rollout feedback. We evaluate DMT-Attack against pi_0.5 and OpenVLA-OFT across four LIBERO suites. DMT-Attack achieves a macro clean-conditioned attack success rate (cASR) of 57.9%, compared with 44.7% for UPA-RFAS and 19.7% for random natural textures. It also attains higher scene similarity and a lower clean-calibrated detector flag rate (45% versus 97.5-100% for optimized patch baselines). These results demonstrate that natural-looking textures can disrupt VLA visual feedback and closed-loop decision-making, motivating defenses that jointly assess visual appearance and task behavior.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Revising every answer from a long-term memory can consume substantial inference budget and damage facts that were already correct. We introduce MemMend, a modular selective revision framework that routes potentially incomplete answers to an editor, represents corrections as bounded exact-text replacements, and accepts only edits with resolvable source coordinates and valid edit operations. The editor preserves the current answer when no legal repair is proposed; accepted edits require no additional semantic-verification call. On 1,986 LoCoMo development questions, MemMend achieves 67.17 conversation-macro F1 and 88.35 mean Judge score, improving over a verified revision chain by 0.82 and 1.24 points while using 62.37% fewer revision calls. Routing uses 54.15% fewer revision calls than ungated local review, with similar F1 but a 0.67-point lower Judge score. Applied directly to native PropMem, Mem0, and SimpleMem answers, the editing interface raises F1 by 3.79, 6.44, and 0.42 points. A source-by-editing factorial supports local editing for F1 but leaves an independent quality benefit from source obligations unresolved. Transfer to 451 LongMemEval-V2 Small questions also favors revision efficiency, while an external PerLTQA dialogue subset reveals a completeness gap. These results support selective editing as a reusable quality-cost operating point.

Abstract (英文): Retrieval-based classifiers efficiently route queries to specialised services, but they typically select a candidate even when no suitable service exists. We introduce Adaptive Decision and Abstention with Pool-Size-Aware Thresholds for Service Routing (ADAPT), a lightweight routing architecture with an explicit fallback option that enables queries to abstain from specialist selection. ADAPT uses the separation between the highest relevance score and the candidate score distribution as routing confidence. Experiments across retrieval models and increasing numbers of services reveal a consistent empirical behaviour: the confidence distribution changes systematically with candidate-pool size K, making fixed abstention thresholds increasingly miscalibrated. ADAPT addresses this effect with a K-aware threshold that adapts as services are added without classifier retraining. Evaluation across two specialist datasets shows that this simple mechanism provides an effective accuracy-cost trade-off compared with generative LLM routing, supporting lightweight and dynamically extensible service-routing architectures.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Full-duplex interaction models exhibit strong conversational agency, enabling natural, low-latency turn-taking, backchannels, and interruptions. However, completing tasks further requires task agency: timely tool invocation and argument revision following user corrections. Frontend-backend agents separate initiation from execution: the frontend decides whether and what to request, while the backend determines how. Execution can be offloaded, but unsent requests cannot be executed, and incomplete ones can misdirect the backend. On Full-Duplex-Bench v3 (FDB v3), most failures of Realtime-Venus-Audio + Harness in our streaming pipeline occur before a domain tool executes. We introduce Duopra, a full-duplex interaction model that learns task execution alongside conversational control. Duopra treats the backend as a tool, accepting a self-contained natural-language request. Direct calls and backend delegation share an action interface and requirements for faithful initiation and correct execution. Its three-stage training combines protocol SFT, full-duplex RL rewarding timely and complete execution, and projector-only repair of talker conditioning after thinker updates. With the same backend, Pass@1 rises from 4.7% to 44.7%; Duopra achieves 47.7% calling tools directly (26.0% after SFT) and 49.3% with a DeepSeek backend. It improves three FDB v1.0 takeover rates over the base model. These results show that task agency can coexist with key conversational agency.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal safety guards increasingly generate rationales alongside safety predictions, yet whether these rationales are reliable remains largely unexplored. We systematically study *rationale hallucination* from two perspectives: visual rationale hallucination, where rationales contain unsupported visual facts, and safety attribution hallucination, where grounded content is assigned an incorrect safety interpretation. Across rationale-generation methods, multiple vision-language backbones, and safety datasets, we find that rationale hallucination is widespread and does not consistently diminish with higher classification accuracy or increased model scale. Further analysis of the Think-with-Image paradigm shows that hallucination is concentrated in fine-grained attributes and relations, while visual tools are often invoked infrequently or without sufficient spatial focus. Based on these findings, we propose *Tool-Contrastive Grounding Reinforcement Learning* (TCG-RL), which rewards tool use only when it improves rationale grounding over a tool-free reference. TCG-RL substantially reduces rationale hallucination while maintaining competitive safety prediction performance. By revealing the gap between prediction correctness and rationale reliability, our study motivates a shift in multimodal safety guarding from outcome-only evaluation toward safety decisions that are both accurate and verifiably grounded.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many prompt-driven FLUX editing methods rely on injecting source features into target-conditioned generation, creating an inherent trade-off between source preservation and the desired edit. We address this problem by controlling where source features are injected and which transformer blocks are used. First, we introduce adaptive masks that spatially gate feature injection using differences between source- and target-conditioned block outputs at matched inversion states. Second, we use REINFORCE to select Top-K injection blocks under different preservation-editability objectives. The selected blocks are fixed at inference and require no online search. On PIE-Bench with FLUX.1-dev, spatial masking controls the alignment-preservation trade-off. On 60 validation examples, a learned nine-block set gives lower whole-image LPIPS than the 18-block RF-Edit set within the same masked pipeline, with slightly lower alignment. Among 50 random nine-block sets, it has the lowest LPIPS but not the highest reward. Separate objective sweeps reveal different block preferences for preservation and alignment.

Abstract (英文): Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. We study a small, fully specified reconciliation contract: commit the target model's greedy answer (the anchor), draw one or two greedy checks that reach the answer by a different route (an alternate prompt, or a model from another family), and let a pure function return one of five outcomes, of which only Agree is acted on. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As complete pipelines, the point estimates of the three-call contract and pooled semantic entropy over 3 or 5 samples differ by less than one point, with intervals containing zero. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Whether large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are a form of life is a scientifically interesting question. Under the Darwinian definition of life, it is also a practical one, because a system capable of Darwinian Evolution can adapt. We therefore test several modes in which a language model can reproduce itself: by emitting a genome that trains its offspring, by copying and mutating its own weights, and by teaching a child from its own samples. In principle, small language models can replicate and evolve: selection improves them, and they adapt to narrow niches. Selection also lowers mutation rates, and a language model, unlike any organism, can lower its own to zero, which may limit its evolution. Flagship LLMs can write such replicators, but for a large LLM to replicate by teaching its offspring remains, for now, technically and economically infeasible.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI systems can harm people, yet the AI actors that provide or deploy them are rarely obliged to disclose these risks. US securities regulation is an exception. Companies registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) must disclose their material risks, including those of their AI systems, in filings that carry liability for misstatement. Materiality, however, is defined by what a reasonable investor would consider important, so harms to third parties enter disclosure only insofar as they become financial risks for the company. We test whether AI harms do so by matching 9,001 AI incidents to 18,229 SEC filings of 328 AI actors by risk type. Of the 1,901 incidents with filings before and after them, half belong to a risk type that the AI actor never disclosed, and this share varies with the kind of harm. Filings disclose the risk types of 67\% of system failures but only 2\% of social system harms, and AI actors themselves report fewer than 2\% of incidents. Securities disclosure thus records the risks that AI actors face rather than the harms that people subject to AI bear. Public authorities that hold AI actors accountable for such harms could make them financially material. We release DISC, the Disclosure--Incident Securities Corpus, to support this work.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Personalized LLM agents must act on a user's current preferences, which change across interactions and are rarely stated in full. Existing memory systems store and retrieve content from the interaction history and leave the agent to work out which preferences still hold and where they apply. Such content does not indicate whether a preference is still current or where it applies, so agents often act on outdated or misapplied preferences. We instead treat memory as the assistant's belief about the user. Following Bourdieu's account of habitus as durable but revisable dispositions, we model the user as a latent preference state that every interaction, including the current request, both reveals and updates. The belief summarizes what the history supports about this state, and memory consists of the individual beliefs that bear on the agent's current decision, each with its evidence, scope, and status. We implement this view in HabitusMem, a trained writer that states these beliefs as text records for a frozen agent. Across two agent families, HabitusMem achieves the best results among memory systems on both PersonaMem-v2 and VitaBench 2.0. Controlled analyses show that learned request conditioning drives the answering gain, while auxiliary training tasks improve tool use.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Lifelong model editing requires continuously incorporating new facts into deployed large language models (LLMs) without expensive retraining or compromising existing capabilities. Memory-based editors offer an appealing solution by keeping the base parameters untouched, yet their practical use is hindered by linearly growing storage requirements and deteriorating retrieval performance as edits accumulate. To tackle these problems, we introduce BiSpace, a lifelong model editing framework that decouples memory retrieval from knowledge injection across two distinct representation spaces. BiSpace routes queries and identifies relevant edits in an external semantic space, while injecting the corresponding knowledge into the internal representation space of the LLM through sparse and storage-efficient updates. Extensive experiments show that BiSpace shrinks each entry's storage to only about 10.2% of the original and shows strong resilience to long edit sequences of up to 16K edits. On a more challenging benchmark, BiSpace further improves Overall Performance by more than 23% over existing methods. These results advance memory-based editing toward reliable long-term model maintenance at scale.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Traffic scenario understanding requires combining fine-grained visual evidence with spatial and temporal reasoning. Tool-augmented vision-language models (VLMs) can use specialized perception tools, but effective collaboration faces two challenges: terminal rewards provide limited guidance for intermediate decisions and error recovery, while repeated planning adds unnecessary cost across similar tasks. Echoing the fast-slow dichotomy of human cognition, we propose StarTV-Agent, which combines policy learning with experience evolution to support adaptive reasoning when needed and efficient reuse of prior experience when possible. During training, Role-Sensitive Process Optimization (RSPO) combines process advantages with terminal advantages, while Weakness-Driven Demonstration Learning (WDL) provides validated teacher demonstrations targeting the student's weak questions. During inference, Lightweight Adaptive Workflow Reuse (LAWR) enables a fast path by selecting suitable workflows and binding task-specific arguments and resource references, while a slow ReAct-based path handles tasks without suitable workflows. We further design label-free feedback based on execution completion, workflow adoption, and cost savings to guide workflow reuse and memory updates. Across three challenging benchmarks, StarTV-Agent achieves up to 8.0% relative performance improvement, while reducing token consumption by up to 52.1%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The occurrence of echo chambers is a well-known phenomenon in social networks. Echo chambers are groups of agents satisfying three properties: homogeneity, i.e., sharing the same opinion, segregation, i.e., listening primarily to agents of the same group, and reinforcement, i.e., increasing opinion similarity over time. Opinion dynamics models have been used to explain how echo chambers occur through agents updating their opinions. However, social network users also exchange beliefs supporting their opinions. Here, we aim at identifying the effect of propagating beliefs together with opinions on the formation of echo chambers. For that purpose, we complement an existing opinion dynamics model with classical belief merge operations. Beliefs and opinions are also maintained coherent guided by values that agents share. We introduce measures, applying to either beliefs and opinions, to identify echo chambers based on the three properties. This model is used to simulate opinion and belief propagation with varying initial conditions and agent tolerance for different opinions/beliefs. Results confirm the occurrence of echo chambers observed in previous opinion dynamics studies in a different setting and with a stricter measure. They also demonstrate the occurrence of echo chambers with belief propagation. Finally, they show that connecting opinions to beliefs and maintaining their coherence increases the number of belief echo chambers, but that they involve a reduced number of agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Publicly available geospatial data provide increasing opportuni- ties for data-driven maritime analytics and predictive modelling. However, their availability does not necessarily imply that the underlying observations are equally suitable for downstream AI applications. Properties of the data source and observation pro- cess, including provenance, reporting regularity, spatial coverage, observation density, and missingness, can affect how geospatial data are interpreted, processed, and ultimately used by predictive models. At the same time, trustworthy geospatial AI requires ana- lytical workflows in which these processing decisions can be made explicit and reproduced. This paper presents a reproducible mar- itime geospatial AI workflow built around damast, an open-source data-processing and machine-learning library developed at Simula Research Laboratory. Using AIS observations from multiple public data providers as a case study, we demonstrate metadata-aware validation, reusable processing pipelines, maritime trajectory con- struction, and reproducible machine-learning experimentation. The predictive stage is model-agnostic and is instantiated in this study using a Transformer-based vessel-trajectory forecasting model. Building on these implemented capabilities, we further outline an envisioned extension toward trustworthiness-aware geospatial AI. The extension introduces explicit characterization of source- and data-level properties and their relationship with downstream model performance. This distinguishes capabilities demonstrated in the current workflow from trustworthiness-oriented components identified for future development. Overall, the study demonstrates how reproducible open-source infrastructure can provide a practi- cal foundation for developing more transparent and trustworthy geospatial AI workflows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models enable agents to optimize RTL using EDA feedback, yet their executable search procedures remain largely hand- engineered. We present Meta-RTL, an EDA-grounded framework that evolves RTL optimization harnesses from a minimal loop while keeping the optimization model, EDA flow, and per-design budgets fixed. Compared with Dr. RTL, Meta-RTL* reduces aggregate WNS/TNS violations by 7.8%/11.0% across 20 development designs, with area reductions on 70% of them. Without further evolution, Meta-RTL* transfers to 10 unseen IC-RTL designs, reducing aggregate WNS/TNS violations by 20.5%/14.0% and total synthesized area by 12.6%. We further characterize three search mechanisms in the evolved harness and assess their contributions through ablations and a case study.

Abstract (英文): Machine learning (ML) is now a standard tool for forecasting the output of solar photovoltaic (PV) plants, yet published accuracy figures are often hard to compare and harder to trust. Many studies validate with random splits, report no reference forecast, and present feature attributions without saying what they can and cannot show. This paper reviews the main families of ML methods for PV power forecasting and resource assessment, from tree ensembles to deep and foundation models, and sets out a reproducible workflow that combines automated model search with explainable AI. The workflow is then tested on an openly available dataset: a 16.32 kWp plant in Porto, Portugal, forecast one day ahead from a 4 km numerical weather prediction grid. Over a full test year, the best models reach a normalised root mean square error (nRMSE) of 11.6% of installed capacity, about half the error of smart persistence. The choice of input features changed accuracy far more than the choice among strong algorithms, and shuffled cross-validation was mildly optimistic. After conformal calibration, the nominal 80% prediction intervals covered 81.7% of observations, against 64.5% before. SHAP rankings stayed stable when the model was retrained on different periods but shifted when correlated inputs were removed, so attributions should be read as properties of a model rather than of the plant. The paper closes with practical lessons and open problems, including deployment on edge hardware and the effect of a changing climate.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Diffusion language models typically use a fixed denoising schedule across workloads. We show that the cheapest schedule that preserves quality depends on the task, model, and sampler. We propose Task-Calibrated Denoising Allocation (TCDA), a deployment rule with an explicit error guarantee. Given candidate samplers, paired calibration data, a utility tolerance, and guardrails against degeneration, TCDA uses simultaneous one-sided bootstrap bounds to certify which candidates meet both the utility and guardrail criteria. It deploys the cheapest certified candidate or falls back to the full schedule. The probability of deploying an infeasible schedule is asymptotically at most alpha. Candidates include early prefix decoding, state-reusing continuation, reduced time grids, and confidence-threshold decoding. Across seven datasets and two model families, TCDA certifies reductions in denoiser evaluations by factors of 3.4 on XSum and 2.0 on WMT14 for ELF-B; 8.0 on WMT14 and 4.1 on GSM8K for LLaDA-8B; and 1.33 on OpenWebText for both ELF-M and ELF-L. A validation protocol fixed before generation confirmed every selection on fresh seeds. Across 500 calibration splits, false certification remained below 2% in every setting at a nominal level of 5%. Certified schedules vary across tasks and model scales. Trajectory measurements suggest that when decoded tokens commit is more informative for schedule selection than the remaining latent discretization error. We also find that the ELF reference sampler draws its time grid at random, so reproducible schedule comparisons for unconditional generation require fixing the grid.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative models produce many candidate solutions but provide no intrinsic signal by which to rank them. One mitigation is to demand a \emph{verifiable artifact}: a program that can be executed against the available evidence. A common concern is that requiring executable verification incurs costs in both solution quality and computational budget. We measure these two costs on the $120$-task ARC-AGI-2 evaluation set. Demanding a verifiable artifact from an LLM reduced the score from $93$ to $68$ of $120$ tasks ($77\%$ to $57\%$). Of those $25$ tasks, $21$ were reached by no program-producing configuration we ran, at any budget, by sampling or by search; yet for $27$ of the $28$ tasks that only the answer-only arm solved, some program arm held a program that reproduced every demonstration and still failed the test. The bottleneck is obtaining an explanation that \emph{generalizes}, not finding one. A short evolutionary search over two already-sampled programs, at no additional model cost, beats best-of-$2$ sampled code by $9.2 \pm 1.4$ tasks, and over a single sampled program beats $1$-shot sampling by $9.9 \pm 1.3$. Moreover, because a program can be checked against the training examples, any program-producing protocol can terminate early once a task is explained: under the same stopping rule, best-of-$n$ sampling declines up to $38\%$ of its budget and the evolved arm up to $49\%$, whereas an answer-only baseline cannot terminate early. Applying the same methodology to decoder synthesis for quantum error correction yields interpretable decoders evaluated against a certified panel of published decoders on held-out exam shots.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Query-specific rubrics make LLM evaluation more fine-grained and transparent, and generating high-quality rubrics requires both identifying the target query's evaluation scope and specifying concrete checks. Historical rubrics provide reusable evaluation experiences, yet existing approaches reuse either complete query-rubric cases or aggregated criteria and dimensions without explicitly connecting recurring evaluation requirements to their criterion-level evidence and source-query context. This can preserve irrelevant task details or lose the broader evaluation structure. In this paper, we introduce RubricDuo, which organizes historical query-rubric pairs into a Rubric Knowledge Graph linking evaluation dimensions, shared concepts, local concepts, criteria, and queries, where reusable evaluation requirements across queries are abstracted by shared concepts. Given a target query, RubricDuo first combines concepts relevant to the target query with retrieved historical cases to draft a rubric with broad coverage. It then revisits each draft criterion, retrieves concept-linked historical criteria and their source queries, and adapts applicable evidence to refine the criterion. Experiments on RubricBench and HealthBench demonstrate that RubricDuo achieves the overall best performance among the evaluated automatic methods in both alignment with human-authored rubrics and downstream LLM judging.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Enterprises are moving agentic workloads from closed APIs to their own open-weight model deployments. Typically, rollouts are evaluated with ad hoc tools whose accuracy is rarely checked. We present an end-to-end evaluation pipeline for agentic LLM serving and empirically ground the design choices that connect its stages. Our experiments show that replaying captured traces reproduces the per-session load of the original agent sessions: at the 95th percentile replay stays within 1.18x of the recorded input tokens while live re-runs reach 4.98x, so a small set of recorded sessions yields a repeatable per-session token load. A code-coverage analysis of vLLM further shows that the evaluated live, replayed, and synthetic workloads are complementary in this configuration: each exercises Python serving paths not reached in the measured runs of the others. In a case study rolling out four large mixture-of-experts (MoE) models on llm-d, experiments show that the target models reached about half the optimal concurrency, and correspondingly about 60% of the throughput, on captured workloads compared with public traces. We open-source the relevant tools and release a trace dataset.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) paired with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become the dominant recipe for post-training LLMs on complex reasoning tasks, but has been shown to collapse the policy into a low-diversity mode. Repeated LLM outputs are nearly identical, and pass@k accuracy converges or degrades as a result. We propose ROAM-GRPO (Routed Off-mode Approach Mining for GRPO), an augmentation to the GRPO algorithm which incentivizes exploration of distinct reasoning strategies and aims to increase output creativity during evaluation. ROAM-GRPO detects well-solved problems during training and transfers them to an "off-mode" track. The model is prompted to produce distinct solution strategies to such problems, which are verified for logical validity and distinctness by an LLM judge and subsequently self-distilled into the policy. Training Qwen3.5-2b on a 1,000-problem subset of the MATH lvl5 dataset, ROAM-GRPO implementation increases peak pass@16 by 4.7% and correct embedding cosine distance by approximately 1.5-2x on a difficult evaluation set.

Abstract (英文): We investigate surface modeling of multi-transparent interfaces to achieve accurate light transmission. The interplay of errors in geometric structure, light paths, and observation results during the reconstruction of transparent media with a high number of layers makes this a challenging problem. Some existing techniques address the reconstruction of transparent scenes by reducing the number of layers or simplifying light paths; however, this limits their direct applicability to high-order transparent scenes. We propose GaussTrans, a compact framework for light transport in high-order transparent scenes, consisting of three components: Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces, Transport-Coupled Refinement, and Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection. Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces use a hybrid approach combining Gauss surfaces and fields to reconstruct different interfaces. Transport-Coupled Refinement reduces coupling errors by evaluating the entire light path. Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection combines primitive support regions to reduce errors in the intersection of interfaces and rays. In experiments on datasets such as RefRef, GaussTrans achieves significantly higher accuracy with relatively low spatial storage requirements.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Crash simulations of glass-fiber-reinforced plastic parts are highly sensitive to the fiber-orientation field supplied to the material model. In conventional workflows, that field comes from resin-flow analysis, which requires molding-specialist support and takes about 25 hours of work per part. We previously proposed a surrogate model that addresses this bottleneck by predicting the orientation field in about 3 minutes. This paper reports the implementation, validation, and operational design that turned the model into an engineering tool for Honda design workflows. We present staged validation from 2D plates to production parts, evaluation against a predefined engineering angular-error criterion, and the expert review that approved design use in March 2025; the system has since been transferred to the crash-safety group and is in initial, limited use in directed fracture-prediction scenarios. The developed system combines injection-gate setting support for cases in which gates are unknown (90% gate-count accuracy, mean position error 11% of flow length), voxelization, surrogate inference, automatic mapping to the crash CAE model, and report generation in a single GUI. The complete orientation-preparation workflow takes about 0.1 hours, a 99.6% reduction from the conventional 25-hour flow-analysis route, and can be run directly by crash CAE engineers without molding-specialist support. As evidence of practical impact, component-level crash analysis using the predicted orientation field reduced the absolute peak-force error from 19.0% with an isotropic baseline to 11.4%, while confining predicted fracture to the test-observed region. The deployment lesson is that a surrogate model alone is not enough for practical adoption: gate-setting support, automatic data conversion, and shareable reporting are what make the model usable as a design tool.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Simulation-driven design of real engineering components has to meet three conditions at once. It runs on few solves, since a nonlinear finite-element simulation of a 3D part takes hours; its design space has many values, the hundreds to thousands of values of the part's CAD model; and a better design often needs a redesign, coordinated changes of the part's proportions rather than local polishing of its surface. Automated optimisers meet at most two of these conditions. Classical optimisers, Bayesian or derivative-free, need a person to cut the design down to a few parameters, and on all the values they need far more solves; industrial shape optimisers move every surface node of the mesh in one solve per cycle, but only in small steps that polish rather than redesign. We ask whether a general-purpose large language model (LLM) can meet all three by joining what it knows with what the simulation shows. GPT-6 Astra edits a magnesium stent through a CAD geometry parametrisation of about 1,650 values, with a budget of 15 simulations; every proposal is built in CAD, meshed and simulated with large-deformation plasticity and contact, and the LLM reads the full results of every solve. Within that budget it lowered the objective, a high percentile of the strains in the stent's bends, by 32%; on the same budget, the industrial shape optimiser Tosca improved the original design by a tenth and then stopped improving. An earlier GPT-6 Astra design, 25% lower in the same objective, was checked in an independent, higher-fidelity model of the full deployment with crimping and damage: it was 14% less strained than the original after the full chain, and its most damaged spot began to fail later and carried 63% less damage at full expansion. LLMs can thus join their learned knowledge with simulation data to adjust more than a thousand design values and reach valid designs with real improvements.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reasoning models provide strong multimodal task understanding but struggle with sub-second control because deliberation increases decision latency. Fast decision models can meet the response window, yet prompting and few-shot examples do not readily provide the fine-grained experience required by a new task. Inspired by coached motor learning, this paper presents System-One Recursive Self-Improvement (System-One RSI), which couples two loops. The high-frequency online inner loop is an Observe, Orient, Decide, Act (OODA) cycle: a decision model maps each visual observation and task question to structured judgments, and a decision tree converts them into timed actions without invoking a reasoning model. The offline outer loop is a recursive improvement cycle carried out by a team of reasoning agents. The team revises the decision-model prompt and decision tree, captures interaction records, derives supervision from those records, and trains or deploys updated model parameters. The improved system then returns to the environment and generates the experience for the next round, consolidating learned distinctions as external muscle memory. The framework operates with a small number of real episodes, without expert demonstrations, environment modification, sandboxing, or parallel simulation. Experiments in Snake, Zenless Zone Zero, and Black Myth: Wukong use a 27B decision model. Mean Snake score increases from 1.30 to 7.35. In an evaluated scenario of Zenless Zone Zero, median completion time decreases by 8.4%. In an evaluated combat scenario of Black Myth: Wukong, the evasive policy achieves 11 times the mean survival time of a stationary reference, while the offensive policy win rate rises from 0% to 66.7% across the evaluated configurations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluating whether artificial agents behave like humans requires comparing not just average outcomes but entire behavioral distributions across diverse contexts. We propose a stratified framework that computes nonparametric discrepancy statistics within contextual cells, and discovers subgroup rules identifying where agent behavior departs from human play. We show that session-level metrics dominated by win/loss outcomes offer little signal beyond mean comparisons, whereas process-level metrics such as move efficiency reveal genuine behavioral differences. Applied to a commercial match-3 game, it uncovers patterns invisible to aggregate metrics: a playtesting bot is diverging substantially from humans in certain game modes, while showing no detectable divergence in others. We release our implementation as open source to support reproducibility and adoption.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-form video understanding, as a fundamental yet crucial vision task, is being revolutionized by multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based video agents. However, the retrieval-driven nature inherent to these agents creates a significant bottleneck, forcing them to reactively fetch clips based on local saliency without a global temporal plan. To address this, we present ForeSeek, a foresight-driven video agent that transforms long-video reasoning from reactive retrieval into predictive exploration. Rather than blindly probing the timeline, ForeSeek forms visually grounded expectations over unseen temporal regions, verifies them through targeted observations, and continuously refines its search based on emerging evidence, where failed expectations serve as informative signals for principled backtracking. Furthermore, we develop a two-stage training framework that equips MLLMs with foresight-guided reasoning and efficient decision making. Extensive experiments on five long-video benchmarks demonstrate that ForeSeek consistently improves the efficiency--accuracy trade-off of long-video reasoning, achieving competitive or superior accuracy while observing $2.5$--$4.4\times$ fewer frames than the strongest search-based agent. Code is available at https://github.com/harry0021/ForeSeek

</details>

Abstract (英文): Accurate item probabilities can produce inaccurate group-level decisions. A system may score each item separately, yet act on whether enough hidden items succeed: show a slate, defer a panel decision, or inspect a batch. When items share an uncertain latent state, independent marginalization preserves means but removes dependence from the aggregate posterior. We call this error the serialization tax. For binary items sharing one success rate, classical convex-order comparisons give a more dispersed exact count law than any independent completion with the same total mean. We quantify variance and total-variation gaps, derive threshold separation under margin conditions, and connect posterior approximation to decision regret. Separate results cover non-binary outcomes, heterogeneous items, and sufficient posterior resolution. TaxScore identifies large hidden blocks with unresolved uncertainty near a decision boundary for routing to richer posterior heads. On pre-specified, hidden-target-free MovieLens 1M/10M audits, existing shared-latent heads improve high-score negative log-likelihood (NLL)/utility from 0.673/0.696 to 0.629/0.723 and from 0.670/0.691 to 0.639/0.715. Routing gives 0.006--0.012 full-population utility gains, with matched controls identifying their sources. Our code is available at https://github.com/SiminChang/Serialization-Tax-in-Shared-Latent-Exchangeable-Decisions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Camera-equipped glasses can convert an examination page into a query to a vision-language model (VLM). Device detection addresses possession; a complementary question is whether an examination document can reduce the usefulness of an unauthorized capture. We present SEOYUN, an application-specific framework for refusal-inducing exam documents, with optical and encoder-level interventions as extensions. We use reverse jailbreaking to describe the direction of the objective: inducing abstention rather than bypassing refusal. Refusal induction and defensive visual prompt injection already have precedents; our contribution is a threat model, analytical limits, and an evaluation protocol for examination use. We distinguish refusal from correct-answer leakage, show why removing an added dummy option is not itself a reduction in the original answer space, and derive a sampling comparison under explicitly stated camera assumptions. The protocol separates digital, simulated, and physical inputs and requires held-out evaluation after subject-specific selection. A local CSAT candidate corpus and rendering harness have been prepared, but this version reports no model-performance measurements, physical validation, or human-subject results. Accordingly, SEOYUN is a research framework, not a validated defense or a basis for allegations against individuals.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The European Union's Artificial Intelligence Act (AI Act) entered into force in August 2024, and its obligations apply in stages. Obligations for providers of general-purpose AI models have applied since August 2025, and transparency rules since August 2026. These obligations affect developers who share and deploy models and applications on public platforms such as Hugging Face (HF), the largest platform for distributing AI artifacts. However, little is known about how practitioners respond to the regulation in practice. To address this gap, we conducted an empirical study of explicit references to the AI Act on HF. We collected over five million records, including repository cards, discussions, and commits from Models, Datasets, and Spaces, and identified over 1,500 records that explicitly mention the Act. Two coders manually labeled these records using a primarily closed coding approach, and we analyzed the resulting categories per source. We find that explicit references to the AI Act remain rare across all repository types. Repository cards mainly contain claims of compliance and responsibility, like ensuring compliance or assigning responsibility to downstream users, and some claim that the Act does not apply because the model is not a general-purpose AI model. Discussions are used to report alleged violations of the Act, while commits mainly update artifacts that support compliance. Overall, compliance statements focus on who is responsible rather than how compliance is achieved. We believe that this calls for direct platform support, e.g., automated checks and documentation templates, as well as clearer guidance on the scope of the AI Act.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Artificial Spiking Neural Networks have gained remarkable attention in the last few years due to their proven ability in simulating biological neural cultures and producing similar activity and trends. Nevertheless, whether they can be trained for humanoid locomotion while retaining biological plausibility and robust performance at once remains an open question. We study this problem by proposing a biologically inspired spiking network, trained by different learning schemes and tested on the H1-2 Unitree Humanoid. These schemes differ in how recurrent weights are trained. Ordered from most to least biologically faithful, they are: a fixed reservoir mimicking an in-vitro neural culture, where recurrent weights are kept frozen and only a linear readout is trained, the same network but with its synapses trained by reward-modulated spike timing dependent plasticity and eligibility propagation, and finally backpropagation through time as a non biological baseline for comparison. All schemes relied on distillation of a reinforcement learning expert policy with dataset aggregation. We show that e-prop reached 100% survival rate matching backpropagation through time, whereas both the fixed reservoir and the reward-modulated STDP, though the closest to biological cultures, plateaued at a much lower performance (~43.7% and ~11% survival). We explore in further detail which trends a real culture could and could not produce until now. Finally we demonstrate brief bipedal stepping on a full size humanoid H1-2.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study data-sharing among firms competing in the same market through the quality of their predictive models. We model firms' profit through two main components, precision and market share, where the former depends on the firm's own number of datapoints and the latter depends on the endowments of all firms. We characterize Pareto-optimal contracts as those in which at least one firm receives all of the other firms' datapoints. We then propose a parametric family of data-sharing contracts and show that, under any of them, the relative market shares of all firms remain constant, eliminating the negative effect of sharing data with competitors and equalizing all firms' gains relative to the no-sharing scenario. We further show that one contract within this family satisfies both Pareto-optimality and strategyproofness against firms attempting to under-declare the size of their initial endowment. Finally, using a real-world dataset, we empirically test the gain in profit for all firms under the contract described above, and show that data-sharing is particularly beneficial when the machine learning task is harder or when firms' initial endowments are closer to homogeneous.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Active urban sensing orchestrates a mobile sensor fleet to proactively inspect a dynamic road network and gather critical information for system management. This problem simultaneously requires understanding the complex spatio-temporal evolution and making decisions with partial observations. In this work, we focus on active sensing in a large-scale urban road network with utilities grounded in dynamic traffic states, while prior work has rarely considered these factors jointly. We propose \emph{SenseAlign}, a Reinforcement Learning-based (RL) framework that learns decision-aligned predictive representations for coordinating an urban sensing drone fleet. SenseAlign first trains a privileged teacher agent that exploits future ground-truth traffic for optimal sensing choices. The student agent has an auxiliary branch that learns to predict future traffic with partial observations, in addition to the RL-trained actor and critic branches. During student learning, our framework matches the action distributions of the teacher under ground-truth and student-predicted future traffic, while keeping the teacher's observations unchanged. Such a design shapes the latent space for decision-making instead of prediction alone. At inference time, the student agent has no overhead as all this learning is done in training. We conduct comprehensive experiments with the real road network of central Barcelona containing 1570 road segments, and demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms the baselines on multiple utility formulations. The codebase will be made publicly available upon paper acceptance.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Post-training recursive self-improvement (RSI) iteratively improves a target capability, operationalized by a fixed anchor benchmark, through repeated intervention, training, and evaluation. Yet most loops use the anchor's aggregate score as their sole observation, so early gains give way to stalled progress and repetitive exploration. When this anchor-only feedback ceases to reveal actionable gaps, a plateau may reflect an exhausted observation rather than an exhausted capability. We introduce adaptive observation (AdaObs), an observation-side mechanism of harness evolution that keeps the anchor objective fixed while adapting the active observation. At a plateau, a controller refines diagnostic granularity, escalates difficulty, and adds related tasks; validated increments are fused back into training, while every checkpoint remains evaluated against the anchor. Across eight independently optimized anchors in long-context reading comprehension and instruction following, AdaObs raises the mean score from $66.4$ to $73.7$ under a matched data budget, outperforming FixedObs, the anchor-only baseline. The evolved harness further improves the seven-task average on the independent PostTrainBench across two agent backends. These results identify observation adaptation as a missing mechanism of harness evolution for sustaining post-training RSI.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Operations research (OR) relies on algorithms that experts tailor to each problem: a new routing, scheduling or network design problem can take weeks of specialist work before it is solved well at scale. Large language model (LLM) agents now write, test and improve code, but whether they can design algorithms that scale to realistic instances is open: in the FrontierOR benchmark's one-shot evaluation, the strongest model matches Gurobi, with an objective within 1% of Gurobi's and no slower, on only 39% of the large instances. The FrontierOR Challenge asks teams to build agentic workflows that design, test, and improve algorithms for many optimization problems at once. Stage 1 releases 60 of the benchmark's 180 audited problems, spanning six application categories and five formulation types, in weekly rounds of 15; Stage 2 introduces new problems outside the benchmark, with unseen instances. Two tracks are ranked separately: in the Main track we run the submitted algorithms in a network-isolated sandbox with open-source dependencies only; in the Self-hosted track teams solve the instances on their own machines, with any software, within a timed window. Every solution is checked for feasibility, and a feasible solution scores the ratio of its objective to a frozen known reference, averaged over hidden instances.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Developing generalizable, data-efficient machine learning (ML) models for advanced manufacturing remains challenging because experimental datasets are limited and process-property relationships vary across materials and operating conditions. In laser powder bed fusion (LPBF), melt pool width, depth, and melting mode (conduction, transition, or keyhole) govern process stability and defect formation. Conventional ML models typically use dimensional process parameters, which represent laser-material interactions only implicitly and may limit cross-material transfer. This study evaluates whether two physics-informed dimensionless features, normalized enthalpy and Peclet number, combining laser processing conditions with material thermophysical properties, improve melt pool prediction over raw process parameters. We systematically benchmark seven models: four classical ML methods (Gaussian process, support vector machine, random forest, and XGBoost) and three pretrained tabular foundation models (TabPFN, TabDPT, and TabICLv2). Experiments span 316L stainless steel, IN625, IN718, and combined-material datasets, covering regression of melt pool width and depth and classification of melting modes. Dimensionless features yield comparable or superior predictive performance in most settings, with the largest gains in cross-material depth prediction (R^2 = 0.96). Tabular foundation models generally achieve stronger classification performance than classical baselines, particularly for multi-material datasets. These findings indicate that combining physically meaningful dimensionless representations with pretrained tabular models can improve data-efficient melt pool modeling and support more transferable process-window identification in LPBF.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents are increasingly expected to act as AI scientists, which requires three increasingly open abilities: carrying out a given computational workflow correctly, choosing the next measurements from a given design space, and designing their own experiments. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate the first two abilities, in a terminal or in a closed loop that selects from predefined candidates. Evaluating the third ability requires reliably executing an experiment that an agent designs, with readings grounded in a real laboratory. We introduce TwinLab-Bench, which evaluates AI scientists on self-designed experiments in 52 tasks from laboratories in the physical sciences, the life sciences, and engineering. The benchmark supports more than 100 experimental action types, and the agent's report is scored by comparison with a hidden answer taken from real laboratory records. Each task has its own TwinLab agent, an agent that serves as the environment and executes the experiments that the tested agent designs, reading the raw materials of the laboratory and calling calculators that are built from its records. Compared with a language model that sees only the task description, the TwinLab agent almost eliminates readings that deviate from the real records and inconsistent answers to a repeated request, and closed-book probes control for answers recalled from memory. We find that these tasks remain hard for frontier models: the strongest, Claude Fable 5.2, averages a score of only 45.0, and we conduct an empirical study of where it fails.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We present an updated version of fosf, a reasoner for fuzzy order-sorted feature (OSF) logic, with support for flexible SPARQL query answering over RDF knowledge graphs. Fuzzy OSF logic is a knowledge representation and reasoning language where feature symbols denote functions and sort symbols denote fuzzy sets. Sorts are organized in a fuzzy subsumption relation that extends to OSF terms, record-like structures representing classes of entities. The unification algorithm for these structures provides a calculus of fuzzy type subsumption. The latest release of fosf introduces support for similarity-based reasoning by extending the OSF language with a similarity relation among sorts, alongside a translation engine for compiling OSF terms into standard SPARQL. Queries expressed as OSF terms undergo fuzzy ontology-aware rewriting prior to translation. This process incorporates an OSF theory of sort definitions, feature domain and range declarations, and fuzzy subsumption and similarity relations through fuzzy OSF constraint normalization. This rewriting enables flexible query answering by returning approximate solutions, allowing queries that would otherwise be inconsistent with the underlying ontology to yield relevant results.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Textual recommendations generated by large language models (LLMs) can be difficult to inspect. This paper introduces \emph{Client Exits}, a lightweight mechanism for enriching LLM responses while separating semantic intent from client-specific presentation. The model inserts predefined, machine-interpretable markers into its output, and the receiving client decides whether and how to realize the corresponding enrichments. This allows supplementary information to be presented as graphics, text, speech, or other modalities without prescribing a concrete interface. We implement a prototype combining a finetuned Qwen model with a modified LibreChat client. Using the Question--Option--Criteria (QOC) approach including three XML-based client exits. We evaluate two QOC-oriented model variants trained on essentially the same examples, with client-exit annotations added for one variant. Across 100 test questions, the client-exit model includes all three markers in every response. An LLM judge, informed about the mechanism without being instructed to favor it, prefers this model's answers in 66\% of the comparisons. These results demonstrate successful marker generation on the evaluated test set and support the feasibility of client-controlled enrichment.

</details>

Abstract (英文): One fundamental challenge in reinforcement learning (RL) is enforcing temporal safety constraints, such as satisfying formal specifications. Existing methods often transform logic formulas into automata for state augmentation and reward shaping. However, this approach assumes that automaton construction is both feasible and tractable, which limits its applicability to small specifications and linear temporal logic (LTL). This paper proposes Actor-Predictor, a framework that learns a surrogate representation of logic progress without explicitly constructing an automaton. The predictor continuously estimates whether a partial trajectory will satisfy the formal specification in a later step, and its learned features are provided as auxiliary inputs to the actor, which is then optimized using GRPO and RWR. We show theoretically that, under sufficient predictive accuracy, the learned representation is equivalent to the automaton state. Experiments on grid world and robotic tasks in Isaac Lab demonstrate that Actor-Predictor outperforms automaton-based baselines on LTL-constrained tasks. It also performs well on non-regular formal specifications, such as $(a^n b^n)$, for which finite-automaton-based approaches are not applicable.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Laya is a small neural network that reads a text, a question and K written options, and returns one probability per option in a single pass. We ask how its accuracy falls as K grows from 2 to 35 and whether that can be predicted. We score nested option sets (every K-option question contains the smaller ones) on four question pools, for the released model (Base) and our fine-tune (FT), and score every item against each single rival. FT trained on one 12-option topic question (in-family); a 12-option HR (department-routing) question is out-of-family. Logit accuracy (the log of correct over incorrect answers) is linear in log(K-1) with slope 0.68 to 0.88. Fitted on K up to 6, the law predicts K=7 to 12 within 0.6 points and K up to 24 within 1.2 (a log-linear rule: 1.6 and 6.7). Taking the slope from the topic question and measuring only two-option accuracy on HR predicts its other K within 0.3 points (FT) and 0.7 (Base). On a 35-label pool of five schemas the law fails (5 to 11 points). Independence of irrelevant alternatives is rejected: the gold-versus-rival log-odds shifts by +0.24 to +2.07 nats per doubling of K, yet pairwise answers reproduce K-way accuracy within 4.7 points. Order averaging (averaging over several orderings of the options), shortlists, a temperature law and calibrated prediction sets did not transfer (FT's 90% sets cover 84% on HR). 2 of 10 predictions made in advance held. Caveats: one model family and LLM-labelled synthetic text.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents built on large language models (LLMs) often answer questions from incomplete records. Missing information might be interpreted as unknown or false, depending on the completeness of the source. Current evaluations fail to distinguish these two aspects, as they are usually interwoven with other reasoning demands. We present ClosureBench, a benchmark that tests whether LLMs follow the stated rules for dealing with missing information. Every base scenario is tested under OWA, CWA, and LCWA, and only the treatment of missing information changes between variants. ClosureBench provides a verified base benchmark as well as agent extensions for action choice, source scope, and updates. A model is considered correct on a base scenario only if it provides correct answers in all of its closure variants. Across 960 instances, overall accuracy can mask substantial closure mistakes. Models achieve overall accuracy from 71.18% to 99.86% across replicated runs, but their core switch accuracy, measured on the scenarios whose gold label actually changes with the contract, varies between 18.23% and 99.31%. Large discrepancies emerge under LCWA. For example, some agents choose unknown despite the prompt indicating that missing information should be treated as false. Our findings demonstrate that evaluations of AI agents should not only measure whether agents can reason over existing facts but also whether they use the appropriate assumption when information is absent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A one-pass decision encoder reads a text, a question and several named options, and returns a probability for each option in a single forward pass. We ask how far Laya, a 421M-parameter encoder of this kind, can be compressed before its decisions change. We count decisions that differ from Laya's own full-precision (fp32) answer, and track calibration (how well confidence matches accuracy, measured by expected calibration error, ECE), on 1,221 synthetic items with the training schemas, 273 with an unseen schema, and 800 out-of-family questions from ARC, MMLU and HellaSwag. Layer pruning of a related model family is known; we add flips, calibration, held-out data and retraining. bf16 changes 0.2% of in-family decisions but 2.2% out of family; 6-bit grouped rounding 1.3% and 5.9%; deleting 6 layers 0.9% and 9.1%, while ECE rises from 5.9% to 39.2%. One fitted temperature, needing no labels, restores it to 3.6%. One scale per tensor fails below six bits, and CPU dynamic int8 changes 30% of decisions, nearly all through one layer. Total variation tracks flips for rounding but not for deletion, and an early exit saves layers over a static cut only if its confidence gate is first recalibrated. Distilling a 16-layer student cuts its in-family flips from 44% to 9% (45% out of family). At equal stored size, rounding flips fewer decisions than deletion. Data are synthetic and LLM-labelled, for one architecture.

Abstract (英文): Large language models increasingly rely on extended reasoning traces, yet the training signals used to shape this behavior remain coarse. Supervised fine-tuning on full trajectories forces the model to imitate an entire demonstration, overconstraining its output distribution, while trajectory-level reinforcement learning provides only weak credit assignment, rewarding or penalizing whole completions without identifying where they succeed or fail. We propose edit-based training, a general framework for learning from a model's own trajectories via localized textual corrections. Given a sampled completion, an editor identifies the first point of failure under a specified criterion and proposes a minimal replacement span. The model is then supervised only on these edited spans. By design, this concentrates supervision where the model's reasoning first breaks down and aims to preserve its existing reasoning patterns elsewhere. The method applies both offline and online, with the latter continually adapting supervision to the model's evolving failure modes. We instantiate this approach on reasoning instruction following with Qwen3-32B on the ReasonIF benchmark. Compared to full-trajectory imitation and trajectory-level reinforcement learning baselines, edit-based training substantially improves compliance with the reasoning instruction while largely preserving the model's skills on untargeted tasks. These results suggest that localized correction is an effective complement to these approaches for shaping how LLMs reason.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Automating experiments does not necessarily advance scientific understanding. Research agents must identify meaningful knowledge gaps, determine what available data can resolve, and assess whether their findings advance existing knowledge. We introduce DeepQuest, a knowledge-gap-driven AI scientist framework for Alzheimer's disease research. DeepQuest transforms unresolved questions from domain reviews into traceable, testable hypotheses and prioritizes them through data feasibility screening and groupwise Elo ranking. It couples iterative experimentation with two-level auditing that separates experimental reliability from knowledge contribution relative to two frozen literature corpora. On OASIS-3, DeepQuest produces 42 feasible hypotheses and outperforms the strongest baselines in 14 of 15 evaluator-dimension comparisons across three language-model reviewers. The full framework achieves an audit-assessed scientific finding rate of 95.24%, outperforming both general-purpose executors and domain-specific research agents. This scientific finding rate remains stable across literature corpora and evaluator model families. Representative findings concern neuropsychiatric symptoms and dementia risk, longitudinal amyloid spatial stability, and neocortical tau-associated clinical impairment, with key associations supported in independent ADNI and AIBL cohorts. These results show how aligning knowledge gaps, data feasibility, and evidence auditing can turn automated research into traceable, evidence-supported scientific discovery. Our source code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DeepQuest-3066/

</details>

Abstract (英文): Managing concurrent tasks across separate agent sessions quickly exhausts human attention, motivating always-on personal agents behind a single continuous interface. Yet when every context shares identical frozen backbone weights, funneling interleaved dialogues and external events into one linear session induces cross-topic constraint bleed and head-of-line stalling. Conversely, router-ticket hierarchies incur lossy prose handoffs, heavy routing overhead, and blind merges - collapsing true-conflict repository merges to a 33.3% three-run mean despite high isolated branch passes. Rather than scheduling anthropomorphic roles, we introduce ClonAct, a routerless architecture that virtualizes context blocks directly through a pluggable single-pass non-generative Context MMU, in-place Context Cells, a footprint-aware Synaptic Bus with an isolated trial merge gate, and a wave-flushed Shared Trunk. Across three programmatic multi-stream benchmarks and three model families, our portable trial merge gate lifts true-conflict merges to 63.3%, while ClonAct's routerless virtualization attains this ceiling with 45.9% fewer prompt tokens and prevents compact-model router collapse. Under continuous surge streams, ClonAct matches top completion (95.0%) with the lowest median wait and zero timed-out status checks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM agents on long-horizon tasks fail in recurring, procedural ways. They mis-paginate an API, resolve the wrong entity, or answer when no answer was asked. We built a pipeline that lets an agent learn from its own history. It first mines short \emph{lessons} from the agent's own trajectories, one to three per episode. It then consolidates those lessons into a smaller set of \emph{guidelines}, where each guideline records how many lessons it merged. At inference the agent receives either all guidelines or a selected subset. The pipeline ships in the open-source altk-evolve library and is deployed in IBM's CUGA agent and watsonx Orchestrate platform. We report what we learned building, measuring, and productizing it. Our primary instrument is the AppWorld benchmark, across models spanning 40--92\% baseline task completion. The central finding is that the right amount of memory is a property of the model, not the memory. A strong model keeps improving as guidelines are added. DeepSeek-V3.2 gains +9.5 points of task completion and +16.1 points of scenario completion with its full 191-guideline set. A weak model gains less from the same full set than from a small selected subset. gpt-oss-120b starts at 39.9\% task completion. It reaches 51.2\% with all 199 of its guidelines but 55.4\% (mean of two runs) with a selected subset of about 29. Around that result we report what went wrong: a high-support guideline that backfired, provenance silently dropped during consolidation, a swallowed API error that failed every task while producing a plausible log, and a baseline confound that nearly produced a misleading comparison with a prior method. We distill these into recommendations for teams deploying agent memory.

</details>

Abstract (英文): World models are increasingly used to simulate physical interactions, but training a separate model for each environment limits scalability. Cross-environment prediction requires identifying the current physical rule, which appearance alone may not reveal. We therefore study visual calibration: using object motion and interactions in a short video as direct evidence to infer the current rule and predict motion under that rule from a different initial state. However, differences in data availability across physical conditions can yield a long-tailed training distribution, biasing models toward common rules even when evidence supports rare ones. To study this challenge, we introduce TailWorld, which evaluates the transfer of demonstrated rules across initial states while independently controlling training imbalance and calibration informativeness. The benchmark compares predictions with two reference futures from the same prediction initial state, one following the demonstrated rule and the other a common rule, to measure prediction bias toward common dynamics. We further propose Rule Inference for Transfer (RIFT), which learns how different rules affect object motion, infers the current rule from calibration interactions, and applies it to prediction from a new initial state. RIFT explicitly controls the influence of training frequency on rule inference, allowing sufficiently informative observational evidence to support rare rules. Experiments on TailWorld show that RIFT improves prediction accuracy and physical consistency across varying training imbalances and distribution shifts, with gains in predicting rare dynamics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Representative vision-language navigation (VLN) methods typically couple a vision-language model (VLM) brain with a learnable action head and fine-tune the VLM brain on navigation-domain data to predict geometric goals. However, such adaptation can substantially alter base VLM weights and degrade general visual capabilities. We introduce ZeroVLN, a slow-fast navigation framework that achieves effective navigation with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain. Rather than teaching the brain to predict geometric goals, we equip it with a semantic tool-calling interface for conveying movement decisions to an action expert in natural language. We first adapt the action expert to carry out these movement requests, then jointly improve the brain's movement decisions and the action expert's continuous execution through closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). Trained on the same navigation episodes, ZeroVLN substantially outperforms representative SFT-based hierarchical VLN methods on standard VLN-CE (continuous environments) benchmarks, while inducing only minor changes to the brain's weights and largely preserving its general capabilities. To our knowledge, ZeroVLN is the first slow-fast VLN framework with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain, and the first to jointly optimize its slow and fast policies through closed-loop agentic RL in VLN-CE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) can generate Q-matrices for diagnostic classification models that approximate expert specifications, but evaluations have focused on the final matrices, leaving the basis of individual item-attribute assignments unexamined. This opacity limits the responsible use of AI in measurement. We propose Reasoning-Trace Analysis (RTA), a framework in which an LLM generates attribute-labeled reasoning steps through chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting and these steps are analyzed to reconstruct, compare, and inspect each assignment. RTA is intended to supplement expert review and empirical validation. In a simulation study with perturbed fraction subtraction items, four LLMs recovered the required attributes and withheld attributes the items did not require. In an empirical study with 11 fraction subtraction items, responses from 536 examinees, four LLMs, up to three temperatures, and 100 replications per condition, CoT-derived Q-matrices overlapped substantially with the reference Q-matrix (mean overlap .809) but less than directly generated Q-matrices (.877), and they varied more across replications. Inspection of the traces linked disagreements to item-type coding, labeling rules, and how operations were expressed as steps. Q-matrices with similar overlap differed in fit to examinee responses. RTA provides inspectable evidence that matrix-level agreement and fit do not.

</details>
