Abstract (英文): This paper proposes a Noise-Decoupled Multi-scale Evidence-aware (NDME) algorithm to address the critical challenges of feature sparsity and noise interference in detecting tiny targets (occupying <0.1% of image area) in deep-space images. By innovatively integrating physical principles with deep learning, NDME constructs a Multi-scale Quantized Feature Pyramid Network (MS-QFPN) and introduces a Non-local Sub-pixel Attention (NSA) mechanism to effectively enhance the sparse feature representation of tiny targets. Simultaneously, an adaptive noise-signal covariance matrix based on the International Celestial Reference Frame 3 (ICRF-3)-inspired deep-space noise physical model is designed to suppress cosmic-ray noise while preserving weak target features (signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) > 2). Compared with state-of-the-art methods such as YOLOX series and ALCNet, NDME demonstrates significant advantages in small-target detection on the IST-S and IRDST datasets, exhibiting robust adaptability under complex noise environments. Experimental results show that NDME outperforms existing methods in recall, precision, and other key metrics, providing an effective solution for tiny-target detection under deep-space-like sparse-feature and noise conditions, while validation on real deep-space observations remains future work. It should be noted that the public benchmarks used in this work are deep-space-like infrared proxy datasets; they are used to evaluate the sparse-feature and noise-robustness aspects of the method rather than to claim direct deep-space validation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Terrestrial Laser Scanning (TLS) is pivotal for creating high-precision 3D geospatial data in urban digital twins and Scan-to-BIM applications. However, TLS point clouds frequently suffer from severe glass-induced reflection artifacts, which fundamentally compromise geometric fidelity and downstream automated modeling. Existing methods struggle with real-world physical complexities because they rely heavily on idealized reflection symmetry assumptions and fail to address the critical “measurement void” problem caused by transparent surfaces. To address these limitations, we propose a unified cross-modal framework RADAR for robust reflection artifact removal in complex urban environments. First, our approach leverages a multimodal vision foundation model—integrating panoramic imagery and LiDAR-derived spatial maps—to accurately detect glass regions. These initial masks are then geometrically refined and completed via ray-casting to recover missing surfaces. Second, to handle imperfect reflections, we introduce the Reflection-aware Local–Global Geometric Similarity (RE-LGGS) descriptor. By jointly encoding multi-scale PCA-derived structures and spatial orientation consistency, RE-LGGS robustly identifies virtual points even under severe geometric distortion and data sparsity. Extensive evaluations on multiple public TLS datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance across diverse glass-scene conditions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Rapid and accurate assessment of maize straw volume and spatial distribution can help to improve straw utilization efficiency. This study estimated the theoretical maize straw resources of West-Central Jilin Province, China, from 2019 to 2022. Net primary productivity (NPP) was simulated with the Carnegie–Ames–Stanford Approach (CASA) model on the Google Earth Engine (GEE) platform using Sentinel-2 imagery and converted to theoretical straw resources using an NPP–yield coefficient and the straw-to-grain ratio. The estimated maize straw resources were evaluated against field measurements collected in 2022, yielding an R2 of 0.63 and a root mean square error (RMSE) of 849.26 kg/ha. At the county level, comparisons with statistical reference straw resources for 2019–2022 yielded R2 values of 0.84–0.86. The four-year mean straw yield was 9416 kg/ha, with precipitation as the main climatic factor shaping this pattern. In terms of utilization potential, the straw supply could meet the residue requirement of conservation tillage on 87.34% of the cultivated area, and the straw available for feed could meet approximately 70% of the straw demand of the existing cattle and sheep inventories. These results provide spatially explicit information for straw management and regional agricultural planning.

Abstract (英文): Infrared small targets occupy few pixels and often exhibit weak contrast, making them difficult to distinguish from bright edges, background clutter, and noise. Deep unfolding provides an interpretable framework for background–target decomposition, yet weak target responses may be absorbed into background estimates or suppressed during sparse updates. We propose a Target Confidence and Memory Network (TCMNet) that incorporates target confidence and cross-stage error memory into a multistage deep unfolding framework. A confidence-guided multiscale background modeling module combines smoothing priors at different scales with learned background representations. In later stages, target confidence derived from intermediate target estimates reduces the influence of potential targets on background estimation. A transform-domain sparse update module employs learnable transforms and channel-wise learnable thresholds to suppress clutter, together with local target protection to limit the attenuation of weak responses. To exploit information from preceding stages, a cross-stage error memory module integrates current target responses, inter-stage response changes, and reconstruction residuals into a recurrent state. This state produces bounded corrections to the target estimate before it is passed to the next stage. Target estimates are constrained to be nonnegative, while an auxiliary reconstruction loss penalizes discrepancies between reconstructed and observed images. A final prediction head combines target estimates, multiscale local contrast cues, and the reconstruction residual to produce a target probability map. Experiments on NUDT-SIRST and a local 1:1 partition of MDvsFA demonstrate the effectiveness of TCMNet, achieving mIoU scores of 91.71% and 90.31%, F1 scores of 95.68% and 94.91%, and detection probabilities of 98.41% and 96.84%, respectively. These results highlight the ability of the proposed framework to preserve weak target responses while suppressing background interference. By integrating explicit background–target decomposition with adaptive sparse updating and cross-stage error correction, TCMNet provides a structured approach to infrared small target detection in complex scenes.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Higher overall accuracy does not necessarily mean more complete landslide mapping at every location. Across eight rainfall-related events, we examine the benefits of earlier satellite imagery using U-TAE, a neural network that combines images acquired on different dates, as the main model. We compare overall accuracy with the fraction of mapped landslide area detected within local image patches. Mean average precision increased from 0.333 with a post-event image to 0.379 with a pre/post pair and 0.419 with a longer sequence. Adding earlier images to the pair improved mean average precision in seven events but slightly reduced it in Chimanimani. However, the average increase in the detected fraction across locations and regions was 1.1 percentage points (95% interval −1.3 to +3.3). This provides no clear evidence that earlier images increased the average fraction of mapped landslide area detected. The estimated gain in Italy decreased from 3.0 to 0.3 percentage points when the decision thresholds changed, indicating that the apparent improvement depended on how the predictions were converted into a landslide map. The benefit of earlier images was also greater in patches containing more mapped landslide area in some regions, but was not clearly related to the strength of surface change in patches with sufficient mapped area and usable optical observations. An auxiliary comparison with U-Net, a convolutional image-segmentation model, also showed local detection differences alongside higher overall accuracy. Local detection also varied when the same models were retrained using unchanged images. Checking local detection changes alongside conventional accuracy measures helps assess whether adding earlier imagery reduces missed landslide area across locations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) are proposed for operational Earth observation but benchmarked mostly on urban and agricultural data, so their value and limitations for fine-grained ecological monitoring, with fuzzy class boundaries and severe class imbalance, are largely untested. We benchmark five GFMs (Clay v1.0, Clay v1.5, Prithvi-EO-2.0, DOFA, TerraMind) against two supervised U-Net baselines, one with a transformer (MiT-B2) and one with a convolutional (ResNet-50) encoder, for post-classification habitat change detection in Gesäuse National Park, Austria. All models are trained with three seeds on 2013 expert-interpreted labels (23 habitat classes), using very high-resolution red, green, blue (RGB) and near-infrared aerial imagery and a normalised digital surface model (nDSM) derived from airborne light detection and ranging (LiDAR). Applied to 2019 imagery and 2020 LiDAR, their predictions are compared with the 2013 reference to yield change maps with eight transition classes plus no change. All five GFMs are robustly ahead of the convolutional U-Net, but so is the transformer U-Net, which only Clay v1.0 exceeds, by a small margin (multi-class change macro Intersection over Union (IoU) 0.233 vs. 0.209) whose paired 95% confidence interval (+0.001 to +0.045) nearly includes zero. Adding the nDSM helps every model, but mostly in the canopy-cover classes, whose reference labels are themselves LiDAR-derived. Rare or heterogeneous transitions remain poorly detected by every model. Rule-based post-processing defined with the Gesäuse National Park administration converts the predictions into polygon-structured, ecologically plausible change maps that meet the park’s data standards, with little effect on pixel accuracy (multi-class macro IoU −0.03 to +0.04) but lower recall for forest density loss and setback; the maps are therefore suited to flagging candidate changes for expert verification rather than to unsupervised use.

Abstract (英文): Accurate detection of cotton seedlings is essential for monitoring crop establishment. However, manual annotation is time-consuming, and the quality of conventional automatic annotation methods is affected by illumination and background conditions. This study proposes a joint strategy combining green leaf index (GLI) automatic annotation with deep learning detection. Three annotation methods were considered: GLI, excess green minus excess red (ExG–ExR), and hue–saturation–value (HSV). Their nine combinations with three detectors (YOLOv10n, RT-DETR-L, and Mamba-YOLO-B) were evaluated using spatial data partitioning and independent cross-region testing. Under identical post-processing, GLI achieved the highest annotation quality, with an F1 score of 96.18% and a mean intersection over union (IoU) of 89.78% for matched boxes. For initial box drawing only, bounding boxes for 200 targets were generated in 0.399 s, compared with approximately 600 s for manual drawing. Detection accuracy was assessed using mean average precision (mAP) at IoU thresholds from 0.50 to 0.95 (mAP@0.5:0.95). Across three random seeds, Mamba-YOLO + GLI achieved the highest mean source-region mAP@0.5:0.95, at 0.8020. In cross-region testing, Mamba-YOLO + GLI achieved the best detection and counting performance, with an mAP@0.5:0.95 of 0.3446. However, its detection accuracy remained substantially lower than in source-region testing. These results demonstrate that GLI automatic annotation can provide effective training labels for cotton seedling detection. Among the evaluated combinations, Mamba-YOLO + GLI achieved the highest mean source-region mAP@0.5:0.95 and the highest cross-region mAP@0.5:0.95.

Abstract (英文): Adding optical imagery to radar can improve a flood map without improving the flood probabilities assigned to its pixels. We examine when these benefits agree, using a Sentinel-1 radar-only network as the reference for a Sentinel-1/Sentinel-2 fusion network. The same flood processes and reference pixels are compared as optical input is progressively masked. With no additional masking, fusion improves mean flood-area overlap by 0.0391/0.0373 and reduces mean flood-probability squared error by 0.0203/0.0211 on 18 internal and nine held-out flood episodes previously inspected during development. When only 25% of spatial blocks are retained, overlap gains remain slightly positive, but probability error increases by 0.0106/0.0091. Most of the original-optical probability benefit comes from avoiding false flood probabilities on reference permanent water; probability improvements on reference flood pixels are inconsistent. Controls and spatial examples expose trade-offs between missed floods and false floods. Additional single-event checks reveal adverse transfer. The practical contribution is evidence that flood-area accuracy and flood-probability quality must be assessed separately when optical support changes. This retrospective satellite-image assessment relates optical availability to flood-area accuracy and probability quality.
